# Chapter 3: Intelligent Data Analytics for Power Transformer Health Monitoring Using Modified Fuzzy Q Learning (MFQL)

## Abstract

In the smart grid application, the turbine in a wind farm/WECS (wind energy conversion system) is connected with a wind turbine power transformer (WTPT), which increases the generated output voltage of generator from a few hundred volts to a medium voltage level of distribution system. The wind turbine connected transformers face the failure condition frequently. Therefore, manufacturers and users of wind farm projects are trying vigorously to find out the causes of such failure. The main cause of the failure is that connected transformers cannot stand up to meet the severe duty requirements of a wind turbine on real-site, and face the problems of variable loading, harmonics and non-sinusoidal loads, transformer sizing and voltage variation, low voltage (LV) fault ride, protection and fire safety, step-up duty, switching surges and transient over-voltages, loss evaluation, and gassing. So, to maintain the healthy condition of the smart grid application, an intelligent data analytics approach for power transformer health monitoring using modified fuzzy Q learning (MFQL) is proposed. The proposed approach is able to provide the condition monitoring, fault detection and diagnosis (FDD) information to the system operation in an efficient time interval so that operator may do corrective action and/or may plan a PdM (predictive maintenance) for the equipment. The performance analysis of the proposed approach shows better performance.

## Keywords

MFQL fault classification MLP DGA WECS fault detection and diagnosis FDD 

## 1. Introduction

The stability of wind energy conversion system (WECS) depends on the reliability of its sub-systems and all associated components. Wind turbine power transformer (WTPT) is a vital component among all components used in WECS, also a key component in an electrical power network (i.e., transmission line network and/or distribution line network). If any catastrophic failure condition happens in the working scenario of WTPT, it will decrease the performance as well as stability of WECS or power system or may lead to a complete blackout of the area. Malfunctioning of a WTPT is an economic loss, which results in faults such as electrical discharge fault and thermal fault. These faults ought to be monitored for correction and proper functioning of the power system. According to the one statistics of the failure rates for the sub-assembly of WECS, the power transformer in electrical power network (PTiEPN) leads upto 9.92% failures, which is the fourth highest failure rate in ranking after the tower (12.38%), gearbox (10.98%), and rotor blades (10.16%) as shown in the [Fig. 3.1](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0010) \[ ![Figure 3.1 Failure rates for sub-assemblies of WECS.](images/f03-01-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.1 Failure rates for sub-assemblies of WECS.

The root causes of transformer failure are mechanical stress, electrical stress, and thermal stress and these root causes are further classified into two major groups, which are: (1) internal failures (loss of winding, overheating, solid contaminations in oil, oxygen, partial discharge, moisture, and design defects, etc.) and (2) external failures (i.e., lightening, insulation deterioration, system failure, winding resonance, system overload, system switching, short circuited condition, etc.) as shown in [Fig. 3.2](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0015). These root causes are generally generated in the bushing, auxiliaries, tap-changer, and main tank \[ ![Figure 3.2 Root causes of transformer failures.](images/f03-02-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.2 Root causes of transformer failures.

![Figure 3.3 Reasons of power transformer anomaly at incipient level.](images/f03-03-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.3 Reasons of power transformer anomaly at incipient level.

Generally, nine gases (G1:CH[4], G2:C[2]H[6], G3:C[2]H[4], G4:C[2]H[2], G5:H[2], G6:CO[2], G7:CO, G8:O[2], and G9:N[2]) are generated inside the transformer due to fault (i.e., PDF: partial discharge fault, AF: arcing fault, COF: cellulose overheating fault, OOF: oil overheating fault, TDF: thermal decomposition fault, TF: thermal fault, HEDF: high energy discharge fault). Some of them are dissolved in the oil (i.e., G1–G6) and some are not dissolved in the oil (G7–G9) \[ Moreover, the anomaly detection rate of the conventional methods provided by the IEEE and IEC are not upright, and some of the methods lack from healthy condition identification as well \[ 

## 2. Data collection/source

### 2.1. Dataset collection for the study

Total 552 DGA data samples are utilized in this study \[ The number of data samples for each case are as follow: 31 data samples for HC, 83 data samples for z1-z3, 85 data samples are for z4, 39 data samples are for z5-z6, 130 data samples are for z7, and finally z8 has 184 data samples. 

## 3. Proposed approach and methodologies

The proposed approach for the FDD of a PTiEPN has been represented in [Fig. 3.4](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0025), which includes two parts. The PART-1 is related to the data set generation and preprocessing the generated dataset. Here, oil samples are collected from the PTiEPN site in an oil cell, which is tested in the laboratory by using DGA chromatography analyzer that is called GDA analyzer. Here, Kalman Transport-X from the United States is used for performing the chromatography of the oil samples. Then collected ppm level of each gas of each sample is utilized for further procedure in the PART-2 of the proposed approach. ![Figure 3.4 Methodology and strategies for implementation of transformer fault diagnosis model.](images/f03-04-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.4 Methodology and strategies for implementation of transformer fault diagnosis model.

The part-2 of the proposed approach is related to intelligent data processing, which belongs to the intelligent data analytics using AI/machine learning approaches. Here in this part, conventional methods of IEC/IEEE are applied first to check the interpretation capability based on the collected 552 data samples. Thereafter, AI/Machine learning approaches are implemented for further analysis. In this study, MFQL is designed and implemented first then its performance for anomaly detection of PTiEPN is compared with the ANN method. The MLP type of ANN method is designed here to validate the proposed approach accuracy. In this chapter, two types of study have been performed as: (1) without selection of most relevant variables and (2) with selection of relevant variables. The procedure of the most relevant input variables selection is known as feature selection. So, in this study, features are selected by using two different feature selection approaches that are J48 algorithm and PCA method. Moreover, before going to implement these two types of analysis (with and without selection of feature), the performance of the proposed MFQL approach and MLP-ANN method have been analyzed with respect to the input variables coming from IEC/IEEE methods directly. After getting all confidence level of the proposed approach, it can be suggested for the online real-site implementation. 

### 3.1. Feature vector formulation based on standard techniques

In this section, a feature vector of 24 input variables have been generated, which includes the 7 key gases, 10 different ratio values derived from the combination of the key gases and 7% values (derived from the different combination of the key gases) as shown in Eq. [(3.1)](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0010). The feature vector H is used for the further analysis in this study as explained in subsequence sections. 

$H = \\left\\lbrack \\begin{array}{l} {C\_{2}H\_{2},H\_{2},\\, C\_{2}H\_{4},CH\_{4},CO\_{2},C\_{2}H\_{6},CO,X,Y,Z,P,Q,R,S} \\\\ {\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\\text{H}\_{4}}{\\text{H}\_{2}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{2}}{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{4}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{4}}{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{2}}{\\text{C}\\text{H}\_{4}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6}}{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{2}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}}{\\text{CO}} \\right),} \\\\ {\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{2}}{\\text{H}\_{2}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{4}}{\\text{C}\\text{H}\_{4}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{CO}}{\\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}} \\right),\\left( \\frac{\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6}}{\\text{C}\\text{H}\_{4}} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{array} \\right\\rbrack\_{552 \\times 24}$

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si1.svg)(3.1) Where, $\\text{X} = \\%\\text{C}\\text{H}\_{4} = \\frac{100\*\\text{x}}{\\left( {\\text{x} + \\text{y} + \\text{z}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si2.svg), $\\text{Y} = \\%\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{2} = \\frac{100\*\\text{y}}{\\left( {\\text{x} + \\text{y} + \\text{z}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si3.svg), $\\text{Z} = \\%\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{4} = \\frac{100\*\\text{z}}{\\left( {\\text{x} + \\text{y} + \\text{z}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si4.svg), $\\text{P} = \\%\\text{H}\_{2} = \\frac{100\*\\text{H}\_{2}}{\\left( {\\text{H}\_{2} + \\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6} + \\text{CO} + \\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si5.svg) $\\text{Q} = \\%\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6} = \\frac{100\*\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6}}{\\left( {\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6} + \\text{m} + \\text{n} + \\text{o}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si6.svg), $\\text{R} = \\%\\text{CO} = \\frac{100\*\\text{CO}}{\\left( {\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6} + \\text{m} + \\text{n} + \\text{o} + \\text{CO} + \\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si7.svg), $\\text{S} = \\%\\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2} = \\frac{100\*\\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}}{\\left( {\\text{C}\_{2}\\text{H}\_{6} + \\text{m} + \\text{n} + \\text{o} + \\text{CO} + \\text{C}\\text{O}\_{2}} \\right)}$![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si8.svg) 

### 3.2. Most influencing features selection

The most relevant input variable section is a process to reduce the redundant features/attributes from the feature vector so that FDD accuracy of the classifier may achieve as per satisfactory level as well as the computational burden can be reduced for the point of online implementation. In this chapter, two different approaches for feature selection have been implemented to identify the best one solution for FDD of PTiEPN. These approaches are: (1) J48 algorithm and (2) PCA algorithm. The detailed explanation and mathematical implementation for J48 algorithm and PCA algorithm are represented in Refs. \[ In this study, J48 algorithm is implemented first by using WEKA based platform and obtained results are represented in [Table 3.1](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0010), which show the rank value corresponding to each input variable as mentioned in Eq. [(3.1)](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0010). Moreover, the all other performance parameters of the J48 algorithm have been represented in [Tables 3.2](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0015)–[3.4](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0025) and its graphical representation of the decision tree is shown [Fig. 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0030). The nodes of the [Fig. 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0030) represent the selected attributes. The main beautify of the J48 algorithm is that it remove all redundant variables directly and presents the most relevant variables in form of its node. Therefore, after the analysis of the [Tables 3.1](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0010)–[3.4](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0025) and [Fig. 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0030), it is concluded that J48 algorithm select only eight most relevant variables for the proposed approach. The selected variables are: (1) %C[2]H[2], (2) %C[2]H[4], (3) C[2]H[4]/C[2]H[6], (4) %CH[4], (5) C[2]H[6], (6) CH[4], (7) CO[2], and (8) C[2]H[2].

After the J48 algorithm implementation, now, PCA algorithm is implemented based on RapiMiner platform to select the most relevant attribute by using input data matrix of Eq. [(3.1)](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0010). The main problem in PCA algorithm is that it generates a new rank for variable during each run of the algorithm. So, user need to check accuracy level after each run of PCA and then need to remove least ranked variable from the data matrix. Hence, PCA is a lengthy process in comparison of J48 algorithm. Again eight variables have been selected by the PCA algorithm similar to J48 algorithm but selected variables are different as shown here: (1) %C[2]H[2], (2) %CH[4], (3) %C[2]H[6], (4) %H[2], (5) C[2]H[2]/CH[4], (6) C[2]H[2]/H[2], (7) C[2]H[4]/C[2]H[6], and (8) CH[4]/H[2]. 

### 3.3. Transformer health monitoring techniques

#### 3.3.1. Modify fuzzy Q learning (MFQL) based DGA interpretation

The MFQL implementation is based on the unification of the Q learning, and fuzzy Q learning. Thereafter, MFQL is the modified version of FQL, which is represented in [Fig. 3.6](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0035). The implementation of MFQL is comprised into 10 steps starting from loading the input data \[Eq. [(3.1)](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0010)\] till performance evaluation. In the implementation, first input data matrix is mapped as per the fuzzy membership function then these membership functions are used as the input to the classifier. For more mathematical detail of the MFQL implementation for classification problem, reader may refer Ref. \[ ![Figure 3.6 Proposed approach implementation steps.](images/f03-06-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.6 Proposed approach implementation steps.

#### 3.3.2. Multilayer perceptron neural network (MLP-NN)

ANN with one hidden layer is designed as per the formulated mathematical model mentioned in Eqs. ([3.2](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0015))–[(3.8](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#e0045)) which includes weight value *W*^(*I*) = \[*w*[*i**h*]^(*I*)\][(*n* + 1) × *q*]![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si9.svg) and *W*^(*H*) = \[*w*[*h**j*]^(*H*)\][(*q* + 1) × *p*]![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si10.svg) in between input-and-hidden layer, and between hidden-and-output layer respectively. [Fig. 3.7](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0040) encapsulates the whole simulation of MLP-ANN architecture \[ ![Figure 3.7 ANN architecture for RUL evaluation.](images/f03-07-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 3.7 ANN architecture for RUL evaluation.

At input Layer: the computation at the i[th] neuron is: 

Data sample selection = *S*(*x*[*i*]) = *x*[*i*],  *i* = 1, 2, ...., *n*;

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si11.svg)(3.2) and 

*S*(*x*[0]) = 1.

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si12.svg)(3.3) At hidden Layer: The computation at the h[th] neuron is: 

$\\text{Input}\\,\\text{value}\\, net\\left( {Hi} \\right) = z\_{h} = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n}{w\_{ih}^{I}S\\left( x\_{i} \\right)},\\,\\, h = 1,2,....,q$

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si13.svg)(3.4) 

$\\text{Output}\\,\\text{value}\\, out\\left( {Hi} \\right) = S\\left( z\_{h} \\right) = \\frac{1}{1 + e^{- z\_{h}}},\\,\\, h = 1,2,....,q$

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si14.svg)(3.5) 

*S*(*z*[0]) = 1.

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si15.svg)(3.6)  At output Layer: The computation at the j[th] neuron is: 

$\\text{Input}\\,\\text{value}\\, out\\left( {Oi} \\right) = y\_{j} = \\sum\\limits\_{h = 0}^{q}{w\_{hj}^{H}S}\\left( z\_{h} \\right),\\,\\, j = 1,2,....,p$

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si16.svg)(3.7) 

$\\text{Output}\\,\\text{value}\\, out\\left( {Oi} \\right) = S\\left( y\_{j} \\right) = \\frac{1}{1 + e^{- y\_{j}}},\\,\\, j = 1,2,....,{p\\,,}$

![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si17.svg)(3.8) where, *S*(⋅)![image](images/B978032385510500003X-si18.svg) = activation function. 

## 4. Diagnosis performance analysis of standard techniques

### 4.1. Performance analysis of standard techniques without AI

The DGA interpretation of IEEE and IEC conventional methods are performed to validate the minimum and maximum accuracy level of each method (i.e., IFRM, IERM, IKGM, IRM, and IDTM). Based on 349 data samples of laboratory, the interpretation results are presented in [Table 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0030) and [Fig. 3.8](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0045). After analyzing the [Table 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0030) and [Fig. 3.8](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0045), it is concluded that IDTM has high diagnosis accuracy of 70.03% and lowest diagnosis accuracy of 49% with IKGM model.

### 4.2. Performance analysis of standard techniques with AI

Two different types of artificial intelligence (AI) techniques (i.e., MLP-ANN \[ 

#### 4.2.1. MLP-ANN based power transformer fault diagnosis

Based on conventional DGA interpretation methods, five different models have been implemented by using multi-class MLP neural network as listed in [Table 3.6](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0035). After comparing [Table 3.6](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0035) with [Table 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0030), it has been found that MLP based DGA interpretation accuracy has been enhanced as compared to conventional DGA interpretation accuracy. The highest diagnosis accuracy to be achieved by MLP model#4 (based on Duval Triangle) is 93.6% (for training phase) and 92.2% (for testing phase). But overall highest diagnosis accuracy is not desirable. Therefore a proposed method is developed in this study to solve the problem of lower diagnosis accuracy.

#### 4.2.2. MFQL based power transformer fault diagnosis

Based on conventional DGA interpretation methods, five different models have been implemented by using multi-class MFQL under as listed in [Table 3.7](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0040). After comparing [Table 3.7](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0040) with [Table 3.5](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0030), it has been found that multi-class MFQL based DGA interpretation accuracy has been enhanced as compared to the conventional DGA interpretation accuracy. The highest diagnosis accuracy to be achieved by multi-class MFQL model#4 (Based on Duval triangle) is 97.69% (for training phase) and 93.20% (for testing phase). Diagnosis accuracy achieved is not desirable. Therefore a method is developed to solve enhance diagnosis accuracy.

## 5. Implementation of AI methods based on proposed most relevant input variables

Here, in this section, again two AI approaches (MLP-ANN and MFQL) have been implemented based on selected most relevant input variables by the J48 and the PCA algorithm as well as based on all 24 variables as shown in input vector H and the obtained results are explained further. 

### 5.1. MLP-ANN based proposed approach implementation

Based on the proposed approach, three models have been implemented and analyzed by using an MLP neural network as listed in [Table 3.8](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0045). After analyzing the [Table 3.8](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0045), it is concluded that MLP model#3 (based on selected variables by J48 algorithm) is the best-suited model for step-up transformer fault diagnosis, which has diagnosis accuracy of 97.4% (for training phase) and 97.1% (for testing phase). As compared with other MLP models ([Table 3.6](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0035)), the proposed approach gives suitable results for step-up transformer fault diagnosis. The graphical representation of MLP model#3 has been represented in [Figs. 3.9](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0050)–[3.13](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#f0070) for further validation of the proposed approach performance.

### 5.3. Comparative analysis using AI based proposed approach

Two different AI approaches named MLP-ANN and MFQL have been implemented and analyzed for eight different data set used as input variables (i.e., Durnenburg ratios, Roger’s ratios, IEC ratios, Key-gases, Duval triangle’s variables, prepared vector H of 24 variables, selected variables by PCA, and selected variables by J48 algorithm). The highest diagnostic accuracy for the training and testing phase has been represented in [Table 3.10](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0055) for all AI approaches under eight different categories. After a detailed analysis of [Table 3.10](#B978032385510500003X.xhtml#t0055), it is found that testing phase classification accuracy of MFQL approach has higher than MLP-ANN among the all under category#8 (selected variables by J48 algorithm) with 98.06%, which is desirable and higher than MLP diagnosis models.

## 6. Conclusions

In this chapter, condition monitoring of a step-up transformer of WECS using DGA dataset has been presented. The detailed analysis of conventional DGA methods and its classification capability have been presented for six different operating conditions of step-up transformer. The incipient fault diagnosis of a step-up transformer has been presented with two different artificial intelligence approaches (i.e., MLP-ANN, and MFQL) by using DGA dataset under eight different categories (i.e., inputs are as Durnenburg ratios, Roger’s ratios, IEC ratios, Key-gases, Duval triangle’s variables, prepared vector H of 24 variables, selected variables by PCA, and selected variables by J48 algorithm). The comparative analyzed results show that MFQL gives better results as compared to MLP-ANN based other AI technique with input variables selected by the J48 algorithm. At the last, a comparative study of J48 algorithm-based results with PCA algorithm-based results have also been presented which shows the superiority of the J48 method for step-up transformer incipient fault diagnosis.

# Chapter 4: Intelligent Data Analytics for 3-Phase Induction Motor Fault Diagnosis Using Gene Expression Programming (GEP)

## Abstract

Fault diagnosis and condition assessment (FDCA) of three-phase induction motor (IM) becomes important with increasing age of machine. Proper FDCA plays a pivotal role in enhancing motor’s operational life, efficiency and reducing catastrophic failures. We propose a realistic FDCA method for external fault identification for three phase IMs using Gene Expression Programming (GEP). The proposed approach is validated on publicly available real fault data. The GEP approach uses RMS values of 3-phase voltages and currents as input variables for identifying six types of faults experienced by IM and one normal operating (NF) condition. We compare performance of our GEP approach against ANN and SVM techniques. We also test our GEP approach for analyzing these fault conditions with perturbations in the operating conditions and with varying loads. Simulation results and comparison against ANN and SVM reveal that GEP approach is superior in terms of analytic accuracy and has lower computational requirements.

## Keywords

induction motor artificial intelligence fault classification gene expression programming feature extraction feature selection 

## 1. Introduction

Over the last few decades, electrical motors have become an indispensable part of our lives; industries, transportation, aviation, agriculture, or even our day-to-day life. Even in this IT enabled modern era, motors have held on to their position as the prime movers of humanity. Among various types of electric motors, induction motors (IMs) are the most extensively used motor due to its various tremendous advantages. IMs have dominated all other motors because of some very desirable properties, as well as of its industrial, commercial as well as domestic level of applications. Out of several advantages, some of them are low operational cost, sturdiness, high efficacy and trustworthiness, low operation & maintenance (O&M) cost, excellent starting torque, and advanced level variable frequency drive (VFD) availability \[ As a result, several electric motors used for the smart grid applications for commercial and domestic point of view are either 1-phase or 3-phase IMs \[ ![Figure 4.1 Fault classification tree for IMs.](images/f04-01-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.1 Fault classification tree for IMs.

Few of the anomaly condition or faults befalling in IMs are stator side faults \[ 

Table 4.1

 Codification of the faults experienced by 3-ph IMs. 

 
| Fault types || Healthy condition || Single phase || Unbalanced-voltage || Under-voltage || Over-voltage || Locked rotor || Overload |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Code || NF || SP || UB || UV || OV || LR || OL |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

![](images/B9780323855105000041-t0010.png)

Several external fault identification approaches for healthy and faulty condition evaluation, are performed based on mechanical as well as electrical signatures. Some of utilized signatures are as follow: vibration, electromagnetic field, induced voltage, surge test, motor circuit analysis, acoustic emission, air-gap torque, instantaneous angular speed, and motor current signature analysis (MCSA) \[ In Refs. \[ ANN based approaches involve determination of local minima and an optimal network structure, both of which are time consuming processes with an added threat of over fitting \[ In Ref. \[ In Ref. \[ The GEP approach for fault identification is able to overcome all of these drawbacks. In general, GEP is able to garner best generalization than the MLP, SVM, PSVM, WT, ELM, GA, and GP. The main objective of this work is to propose a GEP based technique which can achieve fault classification accuracy as GEP combines the advantage of both GA and GP algorithms \[ This paper has five sections as follows: in [Section 1](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0010), introduction along with literature review is presented. Datasets used in the research and methodology applied are explained in [Section 2](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0015). Proposed fault diagnosis and condition assessment (FDCA) model formulation is presented in [Section 3](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0020). [Section 4](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0035) presented the results and conclusions are described in [Section 5](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0055).

### 3.2. Gene expression programming (GEP)

The GEP is an advance data analytics approach. Basically, GEP is the combination of the advantages of GA and GP approaches. So, GEP has both advantages of GA and GP \[ ![Figure 4.3 Graphical example for GEP and it’s ET associated with mathemetical expression.](images/f04-03-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.3 Graphical example for GEP and it’s ET associated with mathemetical expression.

![image](images/f04-04-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.4 Working steps for GEP approach \[ 

$f\_{i} = \\sum\\limits\_{q = 1}^{\\lambda\_{t}}\\left( {R - \\left| \\left. {\\lambda\_{(p,q)} - A\_{q}} \\right| \\right.} \\right)$

![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si1.svg)(4.1) 

$f\_{i} = \\sum\\limits\_{q = 1}^{\\lambda\_{t}}\\left( {R - \\left| \\left. {\\frac{\\lambda\_{(p,q)} - A\_{q}}{A\_{q}} \\times 100} \\right| \\right.} \\right)$

![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si2.svg)(4.2) Where R = selection range, *λ*[*p*, *q*]![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si3.svg)\= predicted value of the individual program (p) for fitness case (q) out of n fitness cases, and A[q] = target value for fitness case q. For ideal fit, *λ*[*p*, *q*] = *A*[*q*]![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si4.svg) for all fitness cases and *f*[*i*] = *f*[max] = *n*.*R*![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si5.svg). 

## 4. External fault classifier based on GEP

### 4.1. Dataset: training and testing

Most relevant input variables in the dataset have been collected as explained in [section 3.1](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#s0025), we divided data into 2 sub-datasets: randomly chosen from 628 cases as training data set and the remaining 160 as the testing data set. All 788 cases have been analyzed and resulting identification of 7-classes have been presented in [Table 4.2](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#t0015). The GEP FDD (fault detection and diagnosis) model is implemented based on available data sets which serve as input signature during training and testing phases. 

### 4.2. The GEP approach

Our proposed approach has two basic steps as shown in [Fig. 4.5](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0030): reading the input parameters and implement the improvements. The GEP uses simulated experimental results in lab and publicly available online datasets of Ref. \[ ![Figure 4.5 Proposed approach implementation for IM fault diagnosis.](images/f04-05-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.5 Proposed approach implementation for IM fault diagnosis.

### 4.3. GEP model formulation

The GEP model is formulated for the demonstration point of view based on [Fig. 4.5](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0030), which is represented in [Fig. 4.6](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0035). The developed model includes six-binary GEP classifiers, which are being utilized to classify seven-conditions: one healthy condition (NF) along with six faulty conditions. ![Figure 4.6 GEP based binary classifier demonstration for IM FDD.](images/f04-06-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.6 GEP based binary classifier demonstration for IM FDD.

For the demonstration of the GEP FDD performance, GEP#1 (model-1) is trained by utilizing the whole training dataset and segregate the healthy condition from the all faulty conditions. For this operation, the used code for healthy condition representation is 0. Now, GEP#2 (model-2) is trained by utilizing segregated faulty data from model-1 for six faults condition. The used code at the output side of GEP#2 is 0 for segregating the faults conditions “SP, LR, & OL” from the “UB, UV, & OV.” Now, GEP#3 (model-3) is trained by utilizing the segregated faulty data of “SP, LR, & OL” obtained from model-2 for three faults condition. The used code at the output side of GEP#3 is 1 to segregate the faults conditions “SP” from the “LR & OL.” Now, GEP#4 (model-4) is trained by utilizing the segregated faulty data of “LR & OL” obtained from model-3 for two faults condition. The used code at the output side of GEP#4 is 1 to segregate the faults conditions “LR” from the “OL.” Now, GEP#5 (model-5) is trained by utilizing the segregated faulty data of “UB, UV, & OV” obtained from model-2 for three faults condition. The used code at the output side of GEP#5 is 1 to segregate the faults conditions “UB” from the “UV & OV.” Finally, GEP#6 (model-6) is trained by utilizing the segregated faulty data of “UV & OV” obtained from model-5 for two faults condition. The used code at the output side of GEP#6 is 1 to segregate the faults conditions “OV” from the “UV.” As a summary of all output codes used for representation of faulty condition at the output side of the GEP models are shown in [Table 4.3](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#t0020): We have used following optimized parameters for six GEP models: 

| For Fitness function: || Rounding threshold = 0.5, Inner margin = 1, Fitness function = Dual Margin, Outer margin = 2.5 |
| --- | --- |
| For Numerical Constants: || Data Type = Floating-Point, Constants per Gene = 10, RNC Mutation = 0.00206, Upper Bound range = 10, Lower Bound range = –10, Dc Inversion =0.00546, Dc Mutation = 0.00206, Dc IS Transposition = 0.00546 |
| For General setting: || Tail size = 9, Head size = 8, Chromosomes number = 30, Dc size = 9, Genes number = 3, Gene size = 26, Generation number = 1000 (maximum), Linking function = Addition |
| For genetic operator: || Inversion = 0.00546, Mutation = 0.00138, Transposition of RIS = 0.00546, IS Transposition = 0.00546, Recombination of One-point = 0.003, Recombination of Two-point = 0.003, Transposition of Gene = 0.00277, Recombination of Gene = 0.00277 |

The model’s FDD performance of each GEP-classifier model (GEP 1–GEP 6) is analyzed by computing performance measures of the model after modification of necessary parameters. Classification accuracy (CA) is computed as 

$CA = \\frac{No.\\, of\\, correctly\\, classified\\, samples}{No.\\, of\\, total\\, samples\\, in\\, the\\, Dataset}$

![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si6.svg)(4.3) MSE is calculated as: 

$MSE = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}{(E\_{k})}^{2};E\_{k} = \\left| {D\_{k} - T\_{k}} \\right|$

![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si7.svg)(4.4) Where, n = number of data sample, D[k] = target value, and T[k] = model output. Mean absolute percentage error (MAPE), 

$MAPE = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\left| \\frac{Fault\_{GEP\\\_ classifier} - Fault\_{actual}}{Fault\_{actual}} \\right| \\times 100$

![image](images/B9780323855105000041-si8.svg)(4.5) For the demonstration of the proposed approach performance evaluation, voltages and current signature are used \[ We calculate classification accuracy, MSE and MAPE corresponding to each GEP model as per Eqs. [(4.3)](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#e0020), [(4.4)](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#e0025) and [(4.5)](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#e0030) and are shown in [Table 4.4](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#t0025) ([Fig. 4.7](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0040)). ![Figure 4.7 High level implementation procedure for GEP model.](images/f04-07-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.7 High level implementation procedure for GEP model.

Classification accuracy calculation is presented in this section. The overall efficiency of fault classification using GEP was 99.925% ([Table 4.5](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#t0030)). The ET of GEP model-1 to GEP model-6 are demonstrated in [Figs. 4.8](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0045)–[4.13](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0070), which represent the input relationship with respect to the output relationship of the model. The ET is represented by utilizing the mathematical equation and also represented in several computer languages as well. For the point of comparison, the MLP-ANN model and SVM models have also been designed and implemented according to the used similar data of GEP. Both ANN and SVM utilized the RMS value of the recorded signals, and diagnosis performances have been compared. The basic architecture of ANN model is 6-31-7, which is optimized by using GA optimization technique. Moreover, other operating parameters are: value of momentum factor = 0.8, tolerance = 8e-4, Mu = 1e-007, gradient = 9.2202e-6, number of epoch size = 32, rate of learning= 0.025, function at hidden layer= tangent, at output layer= linear function, MSE = 5e-3, and binary output at the output node as represented in [Table 4.6](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#t0035). The obtained results from ANN model are represented in [Fig. 4.14](#B9780323855105000041.xhtml#f0075)A,B for the training and testing phase, respectively, which show the regression value of 0.9511 and 0.88152 for training and testing phase. ![Figure 4.14 Graphical representation of MLP model output for (A) Training phase, (B) Testing phase.](images/f04-14-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 4.14 Graphical representation of MLP model output for (A) Training phase, (B) Testing phase.

But for cases, proposed approach GEP is capable to diagnose all these cases according to the actual fault condition. 

## 6. Conclusions

Fault diagnosis and condition assessment of 3-phase IM is most valuable research in the area of CM and FDD of electrical rotating machines. The MCSA is an efficient approach for diagnosing the faults of IM. This study proposed an innovative method of IM fault diagnosis, using publicly available databases of 788 patterns and 900 samples simulated in lab. For developing an accurate and consistent method of IM fault diagnosis, we have used RMS value of 3-phase voltage and current signature, defined by the Rama Hammo \[

# Chapter 5: Intelligent Data Analytics for Power Quality Disturbance Diagnosis Using Extreme Learning Machine (ELM)

## Abstract

Power quality disturbances are the big problem for the power engineer of the electric power network. Due to non-linear loading condition and other phenomena such as transient, harmonics, sag, swell, and interruptions, the power quality disturbance problems are created, which affect the power demand matching problem. Due to this, the reliability and stability of the power network will effect. In this chapter, a novel approach for power quality disturbance diagnosis (PQDD) is proposed, which includes the model development, real-time data generation, data preprocessing, feature extraction, feature selection, and PQDD. The EMD approach is developed for feature extraction and obtained IMFs are processed through the decision tree based machine learning approach of J48 algorithm, which is used for feature selection. In this chapter, PQDD is performed by using ELM approach and obtained results are compared with conventional AI method of MLP-ANN. The results show the outperformance of the proposed approach with 99.97% for ELM and 98.5% for MLP-ANN based proposed PQDD method.

## Keywords

power quality power system transmission line ELM MLP-ANN feature extraction feature selection J48 algorithm demonstration 

## 1. Introduction

With the increased demand of electricity to maintain the speed of development and growth in the technically changing world challenges the electrical industry to supply clean energy with highly oriented on load management and optimization. This leads to the best management of load, by fulfilling all the objectives, standard at the service end with understanding the demand pattern of various consumer of a power utility. The planned efforts are needed to generalize the demand pattern of consumer to minimize the power quality (PQ) issued. Such prerequisite makes the PQ investigation as the most essential territory of research as of late. Viewpoints of PQ differ at client and utility perspective. This issue opens an assortment of fields of electrical building from high power electrical machines to control hardware and low power semiconductor gadgets, to wind up an imperative region of worry for control utilities, shoppers, industry, gear originator, and producers. An electrical power system consists of mainly three sub-systems such as generation, transmission, and distribution. These three sub-systems are integrated together to supply uninterrupted electricity to consumer at various ends. Electrical power distribution system (EPDS) holds an essential position in entire electrical system. Generally, the performance of EPDS is affected due to several PQ issues. Some of PQ issues are sag, swell, interruption, transient/oscillation, and harmonics \[ To understand the current scenario of PQ monitoring in electrical power system, comprehensive literature review is needed. Distributed generation and transmission plays an important role in power systems and it seems to be reliable but it shows some technical issues. So, these conventional distribution systems are being replaced by smart grid system in various developed countries. Smart grid comprises of the digital technologies that allow two-way communication between the utility and its customers, and sensing various features along the transmission lines makes the grid smart. Power utilities, usually, faces the challenge to manage their complex networks for PQ monitoring and can take intelligent decision to differentiate useful PQ data from the raw data. There are several standards which are used for solving the PQ issues directly and/or indirectly. Some of such type standards are: IEEE-519 (standards for current, voltage harmonics), IEC-61000 (EMC emission requirements applies to electrical and electronic apparatus), IEEE-141 (standard for Electric Power Distribution for Industrial Plants), IEEE-142 (standard for grounding of industrial and commercial power systems), IEEE-446 (standard for emergency and standby power systems for industrial and commercial applications), IEEE-493 (standard for design of reliable industrial and commercial power systems), IEEE-1100 (standard for powering and grounding sensitive electronic equipment), IEEE-1159 (standard for monitoring electric power quality), IEEE-1250 (standard for service to equipment sensitive to momentary voltage disturbances), and IEEE-1346 (standard for evaluating electric power system compatibility with electronic process equipment). To solve the PQ problems, most of industries are implementing several conventional approaches. While conventional approaches are sluggish in diagnosis, off-line monitoring, and provides the time-based maintenance scenario. Therefore, researches are moving toward the implementation of artificial intelligence (AI)/machine learning (ML) based approaches for power quality disturbance (PQD) analysis. Most of these approaches are based on: (1) wavelet transform based PQD analysis \[ These earlier-mentioned all problems can be vanquished by using ELM (extreme learning machine). The ELM has three main advantages such as: (1) high accuracy, (2) least user intervention, and (3) real-time learning (i.e., learning in seconds, milliseconds, micro-seconds). ELM does not require tuning procedure for its hidden layer and its output function is generalized SLFNs (single-hidden-layer feedforward networks). The complete study is organized as follow: 1) the introduction of the study is represented in [Section 1](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0010), which show the main problem and available solution as well as the research gap of the research domain, 2) the model formation and its description is represented in [Section 2](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0015), [3](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0020)) the [Section 3](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0020) shows the proposed approach, which is developed in this study, which includes the feature extraction (in [Section 3.1](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0025)), feature selection (in [Section 3.2](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0030)) and the diagnosis method’s implementation procedure (in [Section 3.3](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0035)), 4) demonstrated results and its discussion are represented in [Section 4](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#s0050) and finally Section-5 represents the conclusion part of the study. 

## 2. Model formation and description

For the demonstration of the proposed approach, a transmission line model (TLM) is emulated in the MATLAB platform to facilitate the interlink of other equipments of sub-system level in the power system network. A 20 km transmission line of 25 kV, 50 Hz rating is connected in between source and 3-phase non-linear load. For the demonstration point of view, the following parameters are utilized for the modeling of transmission line as given:

Form [Fig. 5.2](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0015), it is clearly pointed out that there is some deviation and/or excitation in the signal while operating condition during the fault with respect to the healthy operation. Therefore, these signatures may be utilized for the further study in this chapter. [Figs. 5.3](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0020) and [5.4](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0025) represent the correlation of recorded current and voltage signature, respectively with respect to each other. From these figures, it is clearly concluded that recorded signals are highly correlated with respect to each other under different operating condition. Finally, [Fig. 5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0030) in this section presents the energy evaluation of the recorded signature for each operating condition along with the healthy operation. For the [Fig. 5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0030), it is concluded that harmonics create a lot of excitation in the system so this may be more dangerous type fault for the transmission line. Apart of harmonics faults, other faults also excited the extra energy consumption, which are comparatively higher than the healthy condition. So, the key take away point from the [Fig. 5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0030) is that to identify the behavior of the signal so that these signals may be utilized for further study. ![Figure 5.1 Simulink model of electrical power distribution.](images/f05-01-9780323855105.jpg)

## 3. Proposed approach

The proposed approach for the power quality disturbance diagnosis (PQDD) has been represented in [Fig. 5.6](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0035), which comprises into six (6) sub-parts. These sub-parts are: (1) Part-1: model formulation, (2) Part-2: dataset collection, (3) Part-3: feature extraction, (4) Part-4: feature selection, (5) Part-5: data preparation, and (6) Part-6: intelligent classifier model development. In Part-1, transmission line model is emulated in the MATLAB environment for further uses and connected with all necessary electrical equipments. In Part-2, developed model is executed under six different operating condition for generating the data of electrical power, current, and voltage of instantaneous condition. The data generation is performed for each faulty case as well as healthy scenario. Then generated dataset for each operating condition, are utilized for the further study. In Part-3, the feature extraction process is implemented. Here, EMD (empirical mode decomposition) technique for data decomposition has been implemented, which generate the number of IMFs (intrinsic mode functions), which are utilized as an input parameters to the classifiers. So, here IMFs are the extracted feature for the further analysis. After feature extraction, feature selection procedure is followed in the Part-4. In this study, a decision tree based J48 algorithm is implemented based on WEKA machine learning platform (open access platform). Feature extraction is an important part of the proposed framework because redundant features from the extracted feature matrix may be removed so that computation burden may be decrease and diagnosis accuracy will be increased, which are most important key parameters for any online implementation in real world application. After the feature extraction, redundant variables are removed then training and testing data matrixes are prepared for the model implementation in Part-5. Finally, in Part-6, ELM model is designed and then trained by using prepared training data matrix. After proper training, testing procedure is followed. And then based on testing phase performance accuracy, developed model is used for further application in PQDD. ![Figure 5.6 Proposed approach for PQ diagnosis.](images/f05-06-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 5.6 Proposed approach for PQ diagnosis.

### 3.1. Feature extraction using EMD technique

EMD technique is an advanced data decomposition technique of time-frequency-domain similar to wavelet transform. It is used generally to decompose non-stationary signals to generate the IMFs. The generation of IMFs depends on the two main properties. These two properties are: (1) the total number of local extrema, local minima & local maxima, and total number of zero crossing not differ by unity, (2) mean value of upper and lower envelop is zero. Based on these two conditions, EMD technique is implemented as per the shown procedure in [Fig. 5.7](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0040). In this case study, 16 IMFs are generated for recorded dataset of each case. ![Figure 5.7 Implementation procedure for EMD technique.](images/f05-07-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 5.7 Implementation procedure for EMD technique.

For the better understanding about the difference in between generated all 16 IMFs of each case, the energy magnitude for each IMF of each case are evaluated are presented graphically in [Figs. 5.8](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0045)–[5.13](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0070) for healthy, transient, swell, sag, interruption, and harmonics, respectively. After analyzing all these figures, it is concluded that there is an energy difference between each IMFs for each faulty condition as well as healthy condition. Moreover, highest excitation in the energy magnitude of the recorded signals in harmonics case is highest due to fault. And the lowest energy magnitude for recorded signal is lowest for the healthy case, which shows the energy difference between each type of recorded signal. Hence, these extracted features are used for the further analysis in this chapter. For the detailed mathematical modeling of the EMD technique, reader may refer to Refs. \[ ![Figure 5.8 Energy magnitude representation for healthy operating case.](images/f05-08-9780323855105.jpg)

### 3.2. Most relevant feature selection using WEKA based decision tree

In this section, most relevant features are selected using WEKA based J48 algorithm, which is an algorithm of decision tree. The most relevant input variable section is a process to reduce the redundant features/attributes from the feature vector so that PQDD accuracy of the model may be achieved as per satisfactory level as well as the computational burden can be reduced for the point of online implementation. In this chapter, decision tree-based J48 algorithm for feature selection has been implemented to identify the best one solution for PQDD. The detailed explanation and mathematical implementation for J48 algorithm is represented in Refs. \[ The J48 algorithm is implemented first by using WEKA based machine learning platform and obtained results are represented in [Table 5.1](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#t0010), which show the performance of the J48 algorithm's models. Here, total six models (three models based on current and three models based on voltage signals) are developed using J48 algorithm for feature selection. Developed model based on IMFs of current signals gives the best performance for PQDD, this model is chosen for the feature selection in this domain. For the detailed analysis of the J40 algorithm, all the other performance parameters of the J48 algorithm have been represented in [Tables 5.2](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#t0015)–[5.4](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#t0025) and its graphical representation of the decision tree is shown [Fig. 5.14](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0075). The nodes of the [Fig. 5.14](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0075) represent the selected attributes. The main beautify of the J48 algorithm is that it removes the all redundant variable directly and presents the most relevant variables in form of its node.

### 3.3. PQ diagnosis methods

#### 3.3.1. Extreme learning machine (ELM) overview

The ELM is the FFNN (feedforward neural network), which is utilized for the data clustering, grouping, regression, prediction, forecasting, etc., with a single layer hidden node. It can generate excellent generalization performance and can learn the system 1000 of time faster than BP based NN. It is the simplest AI technique in implementation. The detailed mathematical modeling for ELM is presented in Refs. \[ 

$f\_{Q} = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{Q}{\\beta\_{i}h\_{i}\\left( y \\right)}$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si13.svg)(5.1) Where, *β*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si14.svg) = output weight, i = number of hidden node, *h*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si15.svg) = output value of hidden node at i\-node. 

*h*[*i*](*y*) = *D*(*a*[*i*],*b*[*i*],*y*)

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si16.svg)(5.2) Where, *a*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si17.svg) and *b*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si18.svg) = parameters of hidden node. For N number of samples, output value of hidden layer is: 

$H = \\begin{bmatrix} {h\\left( y\_{1} \\right)} \\\\ \\begin{array}{l} \\text{·} \\\\ \\text{·} \\\\ \\end{array} \\\\ {h\\left( y\_{N} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} {D\\left( {a\_{1},b\_{1},y\_{1}} \\right)} & {\\text{·}\\text{·}\\text{·}\\text{·}} & {D\\left( {a\_{Q},b\_{Q},y\_{1}} \\right)} \\\\ \\begin{array}{l} \\text{·} \\\\ \\text{·} \\\\ \\end{array} & \\begin{array}{l} \\text{·} \\\\ \\text{·} \\\\ \\end{array} & \\begin{array}{l} \\text{·} \\\\ \\text{·} \\\\ \\end{array} \\\\ {D\\left( {a\_{1},b\_{1},y\_{N}} \\right)} & {\\text{·}\\text{·}\\text{·}\\text{·}} & {D\\left( {a\_{Q},b\_{Q},y\_{N}} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si19.svg)(5.3) Assume, target matrix used in ELM is T which includes the actual labeled value “output (o)” 

$T = \\begin{bmatrix} o\_{1} \\\\ \\begin{array}{l} \\text{·} \\\\ \\text{·} \\\\ \\end{array} \\\\ o\_{N} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si20.svg)(5.4) The output weight *β*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si21.svg): *β*[*i*] = *H*^(*ψ*)*T*![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si22.svg). Then the classification will be as: *L**a**b**e**l*(*y*[*p**r**o**c*]) = argmax\[*f*[*Q*](*y*[*p**r**o**c*])\]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si23.svg). 

#### 3.3.2. Artificial neural network (ANN) overview

ANN with one hidden layer is designed as per the formulated mathematical model mentioned in Eqs. ([5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#e0030))–([5.11](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#e0060)), which includes weight value *W*^(*I*) = \[*w*[*i**h*]^(*I*)\][(*n* + 1) × *q*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si24.svg) and *W*^(*H*) = \[*w*[*h**j*]^(*H*)\][(*q* + 1) × *p*]![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si25.svg) in between input-and-hidden layer, and between hidden-and-output layer, respectively. [Fig. 5.15](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#f0080) encapsulates the whole simulation of MLP-ANN architecture \[ ![Figure 5.15 Single-layered MLP-ANN architecture.](images/f05-15-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 5.15 Single-layered MLP-ANN architecture.

At input Layer: the computation at the i[th] neuron is: 

Data sample selection = *S*(*x*[*i*]) = *x*[*i*],  *i* = 1, 2, ...., *n*;

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si26.svg)(5.5) 

*S*(*x*[0]) = 1.

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si27.svg)(5.6)  At hidden Layer: The computation at the h[th] neuron is: 

$\\text{Input}\\,\\text{value}\\, net\\left( {Hi} \\right) = z\_{h} = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n}{w\_{ih}^{I}S\\left( x\_{i} \\right)},\\,\\, h = 1,2,....,q$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si28.svg)(5.7) 

$\\text{Output}\\,\\text{value}\\, out\\left( {Hi} \\right) = S\\left( z\_{h} \\right) = \\frac{1}{1 + e^{- z\_{h}}},\\,\\, h = 1,2,....,q$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si29.svg)(5.8) 

*S*(*z*[0]) = 1.

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si30.svg)(5.9) At output Layer: The computation at the j[th] neuron is: 

$\\text{Input}\\,\\text{value}\\,\\, out\\left( {Oi} \\right) = y\_{j} = \\sum\\limits\_{h = 0}^{q}{w\_{hj}^{H}S\\left( z\_{h} \\right)},\\,\\, j = 1,2,....,p$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si31.svg)(5.10) 

$\\text{Output}\\,\\text{value}\\,\\, out(Oi) = S\\left( y\_{j} \\right) = \\frac{1}{1 + e^{- y\_{j}}},\\,\\, j = 1,2,....,p$

![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si32.svg)(5.11) where, *S*(⋅)![image](images/B9780323855105000053-si33.svg) = activation function. 

## 4. Results and discussion

The PQDD interpretation of MLP-ANN and ELM methods are performed to validate the proposed approach ability to diagnose the PQ issue in an effective manner. The obtained results are represented in [Table 5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#t0030). Total seven (7) models have been designed and executed for each ANN as well as ELM techniques. [Table 5.5](#B9780323855105000053.xhtml#t0030) shows that obtained results based on selected variable excellent performance accuracy of 99% (for training), 98.5% (for testing phase) of ANN method. While training and testing accuracies for ELM case are 100% and 99.97%, respectively. Apart of this, the diagnosis accuracy based on extracted features is greater than accuracy based on raw dataset of voltage and current signature. So, these results show the advantage of EMD implementation for feature extraction for PQDD.

## 5. Conclusion

In this chapter, PQDD has been performed to enhance the electrical power network performance, so that power engineer may maintain the healthy operating condition to meet the uninterrupted power supply to the customer. The non-linear loading condition and other PQ phenomenon such as transient, harmonics, sag, swell, and interruptions have been analyzed, which are the most responsible event to create the power quality disturbance problems in the power network. To enhance the reliability and stability of the power network, a novel approach for PQDD has been developed, which includes the model development, real-time data generation, data preprocessing, feature extraction, feature selection, and disturbance diagnosis. The EMD approach has been developed for feature extraction and obtained IMFs has been processed through the decision tree-based machine learning approach of J48 algorithm, which is used for feature selection. In this chapter, PQDD has been performed by using ELM approach and obtained results have been compared with conventional AI method of MLP-ANN. The results showed the outperformance of the proposed approach with 99.97% for ELM and 98.5% for MLP-ANN based proposed PQDD method. The proposed approach may be implemented for real-side problem diagnosis.

# Chapter 6: Intelligent Data Analytics for Transmission Line Fault Diagnosis Using EEMD-Based Multiclass SVM and PSVM

## Abstract

Online condition monitoring, fault detection and diagnosis (FDD) of a transmission line is required to predict the actual operating condition (AOC) of the electrical power network (EPN). Generally, conventional methods such as base-impedance, traveling-wave, and magnetic-field are widely used as FDD methods. However, the implementation of these methods is not an easy task, which leads to high analyzing costs. In this chapter, an alternative approach to predict the AOC during an online operating scenario has been formulated, which can identify the AOC from easily recorded parameters of the transmission line. For this, EEMD (ensemble empirical mode decomposition) based PSVM (proximal support vector machine), the approach has been implemented which has very high processing speed as compared to other AI methods. To form a linear relationship between model performance and AOC, several ideas have been analyzed. Then SVM is implemented to evaluate and validate the performance of the proposed framework. The obtained results show that the new proposed framework is effective in evaluating the AOC without the need to measure the other parameters except for current and voltage signals.

## Keywords

transmission line power system classification diagnosis FDD actual operating condition SVM PSVM EMD EEMD IMFs 

## 1. Introduction

The enhancing severity of the electrical power network (EPN) and maintaining uninterrupted operation presents several encounters with the fault detection and diagnosis (FDD) of the system. There are numerous reasons behind the blackouts in a particular country. Few statistics for outage cause categories (in percentage) in the United States are: earthquake (0.8%), tornado (2.8%), hurricane/tropical storm (4.2%), ice-storm (5%), lightning (11.3%), wind/rain (14.8%), other cold water (5.5), fire (5.2%), intentional attack (1.6%), supply shortage (5.3%), equipment failure (29.7%), operator error (10.1%), voltage reduction (7.7%), volunteer reduction (5.9%), and other external cause (4.8%) According to Ref. \[ The input feature extraction procedure enhances the burden on the computation of the FDD process and leads the sluggish operation in the online FDD of TL. In the recent development of FDD algorithms and methods in EPN and TL has observed a similar design and development with opting different similar problem \[ Therefore, key-limitations of AI (artificial intelligence) and ML (machine learning) methods for TL FDD exist around in TL’s two-categories only as mentioned earlier (i.e., (1) fault classification and (2) fault location identification). However, for analyzing these two-categories of TL, two different methods/approaches are required individually \[ Latterly, a novel EEMD (ensemble empirical mode decomposition) based on an intelligent FDD model using proximal support vector machine (PSVM) has been proposed and obtained results are compared with standard SVM, which presents superiority of the proposed approach in term of diagnostic accuracy enhancement, reduction in computational time, and programming complexity \[ The chapter is organized as follows. [Section 2](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0015) presents the methodology which includes the model description, brief detail of EEMD, SVM, and PSVM method. Multi-class SVM and PSVM fault diagnostic models are also depicted in [Section 2](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0015). The obtained results are presented and discussed in [Section 3](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0050). In [Section 3](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0050), a comparative study of SVM and PSVM based fault diagnosis is also presented followed by a conclusion in [Section 4](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0070). 

## 2. Methodology

### 2.1. Proposed approach

The proposed approach framework for TL FDD is shown in [Fig. 6.1](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0010), which include all the steps from model formation to FDD in step-wise-step manner as follows: 

*   Step#1: TL model formation with healthy and 10 different fault condition
*   Step#2: Raw data extraction during all 11 operating condition of TL
*   Step#3: Data preprocessing—during preprocessing process, following operations have been performed: missing value filling, removal of spicks, normalization of the data
*   Step#4: Feature extraction using EEMD method
*   Step#5: Prepare the training data (~70%), testing data (~30%) separately and along with its target file in binary mode \[10000000000; 01000000000; 00100000000; 00010000000; 00001000000; 00000100000; 00000010000;00000001000;00000000100;00000000010;00000000001\]
*   Step#6: Design the SVM/PSVM model with its optimal parameters
*   Step#8: Train the designed models
*   Step#9: Test the trained model if training accuracy is sufficient else train again with and/or its parameter value variation
*   Step#10: Save the model for future TL FDD

![Figure 6.1 Proposed approach flowchart for transmission line fault diagnosis.](images/f06-01-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 6.1 Proposed approach flowchart for transmission line fault diagnosis.

### 2.2. TL model formulation

To represent the suitability of PSVM approach for TL FDD in EPN, a 300 km, 500 kV, 50 Hz TL has been designed as shown in [Fig. 6.2](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0015). This model is developed according to available real-TL with all suitable parameters as represented in [Table 6.1](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#t0010). The emulation of model of [Fig. 6.2](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0015) generates the raw datasets for all 11 cases (i.e., healthy case and 10 types of fault cases). In this study, three different categories of faults are analyzed such as single line to ground faults (3 numbers), double-line to ground faults (3 numbers), and short circuited faults (4 numbers). The symbolic representation of each case of faults is shown as: HI-Healthy, LGAG-single line (A) to ground fault, LGBG-single line (B) to ground fault, LGCG-single line (C) to ground fault, LLGABG-double line (AB) to ground fault, LLGBCG-double line (BC) to ground fault, LLGCAG-double line (CA) to ground fault, LLAB-double line (AB) short-circuit fault, LLBC-double line (BC) short-circuit fault, LLCA-double line (CA) short-circuit fault, and LLLABC-triple line (ABC) short-circuit fault. Here, the notations A, B, and C are used for the representation of three lines of a 3-phase TL. ![Figure 6.2 Simplified 300 km transmission line model.](images/f06-02-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 6.2 Simplified 300 km transmission line model.

### 2.3. Feature extraction using EEMD

EEMD is the advance version of EMD algorithm which is use to overcome the problem of mode mixing of EMD approach. The implementation process of EEMD algorithm comprises into three simple steps as given \[ 

*   Step#1: Add the enough amount of white noise ≅![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si71.svg) mean and variance of original signal
*   Step#2: Decompose each new signal (original signal + noise) by using EMD algorithm
*   Step#3: Compute the average of all IMFs obtained by EMD at Step#2 to remove the effect of white noise

[Fig. 6.4](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0025) represents the mathematical modeling in form of flowchart of EEMD algorithm. The addition of noise furnishes the uniform base scale distribution to reduce mode mixing for EMD based decomposition. This procedure increases the accuracy and reduces the false frequency in IMFs. ![Figure 6.4 The EEMD algorithm’s flowchart.](images/f06-04-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 6.4 The EEMD algorithm’s flowchart.

To analyze strength variation of recorded non-linear signals under 10 faulty and 1 healthy condition of transmission line, energy magnitude of generated IMFs of EEMD is presented in [Fig. 6.5](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0030)A–L. ![Figure 6.5 Energy distribution of IMFs of EEMD for (A) Load current Ia, (B) Load current Ib, (C) Load current Ic, (D) Source current Ia, (E) Source current Ib, (F) Source current Ic, (G) Load voltage Va, (H) Load voltage Vb, (I) Load voltage Vc, (J) Source voltage Va, (K) Source voltage Vb, and (L) Source voltage Vc.](images/f06-5a-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 6.5 Energy distribution of IMFs of EEMD for (A) Load current Ia, (B) Load current Ib, (C) Load current Ic, (D) Source current Ia, (E) Source current Ib, (F) Source current Ic, (G) Load voltage Va, (H) Load voltage Vb, (I) Load voltage Vc, (J) Source voltage Va, (K) Source voltage Vb, and (L) Source voltage Vc.

For the modeling of the SVM classifier, the training dataset *S*[*j*]![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si72.svg) and its associated labeled class *T*[*j*]![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si73.svg) are utilized. The class categories *T*[*j*]![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si74.svg) is represented in the form of  ± 1![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si75.svg) and the hyperplane equation can be represented in Eq. [(6.1)](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#e0010) for the dataset dimension d (∴*S*[*j*]∈*R*^(*d*)*s*)![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si76.svg) \[ 

 < *ω*, *S* >  + *b* = 0

The Eq. [(6.1)](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#e0010) shows the best hyperplane to segregate the dataset into two classes and further to evaluate *ω*![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si82.svg) and *b*![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si83.svg) that minimize  ∥ *ω*∥![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si84.svg) such that for all training dataset (*S*[*j*], *T*[*j*])![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si85.svg):

### 2.5. Proximal support vector machine (PSVM)

PSVM mainly used for data classification \[ [Fig. 6.7](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0040) represented the stepwise procedure of PSVM implementation. PSVM is taken as m data points in *R*^(*n*)![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si93.svg) represented using two matrices as matrix Q (*m* × *n*![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si94.svg)) and diagonal matrix G (*m* × *m*![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si95.svg)) with label of ±1. The label ±1 represents the class of each row of matrix Q. The mathematical implementation for linear classifier using PSVM is shown in following steps: 

*   Step#1: Express M as:

(6.10) which is the function of "\\lambda"image and "\\omega"image. ¶ ¶ f(Xnew) = sign\\left( {\\omega' \\ast Xnew - \\lambda} \\right) ¶ image(6.10) ¶ Where, f(Xnew)image = negative, then dataset is classified as class#1 \\left( {A -} \\right)image, and f(x)image = positive, then dataset is classified as class#2 (A +image). ¶ For multiple number of classifications, multiple binary PSVM classifiers are used in a combination subsequently. ¶  ¶ 2.6. SVM and PSVM based transmission line fault classification model formation ¶ Based on proposed approach as shown in Fig. 6.1 of Section 2.1, the TL fault detection and diagnosis model using SVM (TFDDS) and PSVM (TFDDP) is shown in Figs. 6.8 and 6.9, respectively. ¶ Figure 6.8 Classification approach through 10 SVM models. ¶ Figure 6.9 Classification approach through 10 PSVM models. ¶ Both TFDDS and TFDDP include 10-binary SVM and PSVM classifiers respectively which are utilized to classify the 11-condition, that is, healthy condition (HI) and the 10 transmission line faults (F1–F10) (F1:LGAG, F2:LGBG, F3:LGCG, F4:LLGABG, F5:LLGBCG, F6:LLCAG, F7:LLAB, F8:LLBC, F9:LLCA, and F10:LLLABC) scenario. ¶ In the FDD procedure using 10-binary classifiers, SVM model#1/PSVM model#1 is trained using whole datasets of 11-cases to classify the HI condition form 10 faulty conditions (F1–F10). For indicating the HI-condition, output of the model#1 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, faulty datasets (F1–F10) are utilized to train the binary SVM model#2/PSVM model#2 to categorize the ground faults (F1-F6) from the short circuited fault (F7–F10). For demonstrating the “ground faults” condition from “short circuited,” the output of model#2 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, short-circuited faulty data of F7–F10 are utilized to train the SVM modle#3/PSVM model#3 to categorize “F7–F9” faults from “F10” fault. For demonstrating the triple line short-circuited fault conditions (F10), the output of SVM model#3/PSVM model#3 is represented by -1; otherwise 1 for F7–F9 condition. Thereafter, double line short-circuited faulty dataset of F7–F9 faults are utilized to train the SVM model#4/PSVM model#4 to categorize the “F7 and F8” faults from “F9” fault. For demonstrating “F9” fault condition, the output of SVM model#4/PSVM model#4 is represented by -1; otherwise 1. Now, “F7 and F8” faults dataset are utilized to train the SVM model#5/PSVM model#5 to categorize “F7” fault condition from “F8” fault. For demonstrating “F8” fault condition, the output of SVM model#5/PSVM model#5 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. In this way, FDD of short-circuited line condition has been completed. ¶ Now further classification of ground fault conditions, the ground fault dataset of (F1–F6) faults is utilized to train the SVM model#6/PSVM model#6 to categorize “F1–F3 (single line to ground)” faults from “F4–F6 (double line to ground)” faults. For demonstrating the “F4–F6” fault conditions, the output of SVM model#6/PSVM model#6 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Thereafter, double-line-to-ground fault dataset of F4-F6 faults are utilized to train the SVM model#7/PSVM model#7 to categorize “F6” fault condition from “(F4 and F5)” faults condition. For demonstrating “F6” fault condition, the output of SVM model#7/PSVM model#7 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, the faulty dataset of F4 and F5 are utilized to train the SVM model#8/PSVM model#8 to categorize “F5” fault from “F4” fault condition. For demonstrating “F5” fault condition, the output of SVM model#8/PSVM model#8 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Thereafter, single-line-to-ground fault dataset of F1-F3 are utilized to train the SVM model#9/PSVM model#9 to categorize “F1 & F2” fault form “F3” fault condition. For designating “F3” fault condition, the output of SVM model#9/PSVM model#9 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Finally, F1 and F2 dataset are utilized to train the SVM model#10/PSVM model#10 to categorize “F1” fault from “F2” fault condition. For demonstrating the “F2” fault condition apart from “F1”, the output of SVM model#10/PSVM model#10 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. ¶ Hence, Table 6.2 represents the summary of output codification of all developed 10-SVM/PSVM models. The performance measures of each SVM as well as PSVM diagnostics model are analyzed with respect to the model parameter optimization. ¶ Table 6.2 ¶ Fault categorySVM/ PSVM model #1SVM/ PSVM model #2SVM/ PSVM model #3SVM/ PSVM model #4SVM PSVM model #5SVM/ PSVM model #6SVM/ PSVM model #7SVM/ PSVM model #8SVM/ PSVM model #9SVM/ PSVM model #10 Healthy−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLAB+1+1+1+1+1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLBC+1+1+1+1–1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLCA+1+1+1–1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLLABC+1+1–1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LGAG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image +1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image +1+1 LGBG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image +1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image +1−1 LGCG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image +1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image −1 \\Theta   image  LLGABG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image −1+1+1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLGBCG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image −1+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  LLGCAG+1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image −1−1 \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  \\Theta   image  ¶  ¶ \\Thetaimage, not in used. ¶  ¶  ¶ 3. Results and discussions ¶ 3.1. SVM based transmission line fault classification ¶  ¶ Based on the proposed approach (Fig. 6.1), the complete structure of the transmission line fault diagnosis model has been presented in Fig. 6.8 by using the SVM approach. The MATLAB based code has been utilized to design 80 diagnostic models of 8 different categories (i.e., category1: RCL-raw current load, category2: RCS-raw current source, category3: RVL-raw voltage load, category4: RVS-raw voltage source, category5: IMFs of raw current load, category6: IMFs of the raw current source, category7: IMFs of raw voltage load, and category8: IMFs of the raw voltage source data). The diagnosis accuracy of each model has been listed in Table 6.3. The overall diagnostic accuracy of SVM models under category#6 is 99.992% and 89.099% for the training and testing phase, respectively, which is higher than the developed models of other categories. The graphical results for all models are shown in Fig. 6.10. [(6.10)](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#e0055) which is the function of $"\\lambda"$![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si115.svg) and $"\\omega"$![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si116.svg). ¶ ¶ *f*(*X**n**e**w*) = *s**i**g**n*(*ω*′\**X**n**e**w*−*λ*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si117.svg)(6.10) ¶ Where, *f*(*X**n**e**w*)![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si118.svg) = negative, then dataset is classified as class#1 (*A*−)![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si119.svg), and *f*(*x*)![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si120.svg) = positive, then dataset is classified as class#2 (*A*+![image](images/B9780323855105000065-si121.svg)). ¶ For multiple number of classifications, multiple binary PSVM classifiers are used in a combination subsequently. ¶ ¶ 2.6. SVM and PSVM based transmission line fault classification model formation ¶ Based on proposed approach as shown in [Fig. 6.1](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0010) of [Section 2.1](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#s0020), the TL fault detection and diagnosis model using SVM (TFDDS) and PSVM (TFDDP) is shown in [Figs. 6.8](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0045) and [6.9](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#f0050), respectively. ¶ ![Figure 6.8 Classification approach through 10 SVM models.](images/f06-08-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 6.9 Classification approach through 10 PSVM models.](images/f06-09-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ Both TFDDS and TFDDP include 10-binary SVM and PSVM classifiers respectively which are utilized to classify the 11-condition, that is, healthy condition (HI) and the 10 transmission line faults (F1–F10) (F1:LGAG, F2:LGBG, F3:LGCG, F4:LLGABG, F5:LLGBCG, F6:LLCAG, F7:LLAB, F8:LLBC, F9:LLCA, and F10:LLLABC) scenario. ¶ In the FDD procedure using 10-binary classifiers, SVM model#1/PSVM model#1 is trained using whole datasets of 11-cases to classify the HI condition form 10 faulty conditions (F1–F10). For indicating the HI-condition, output of the model#1 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, faulty datasets (F1–F10) are utilized to train the binary SVM model#2/PSVM model#2 to categorize the ground faults (F1-F6) from the short circuited fault (F7–F10). For demonstrating the “ground faults” condition from “short circuited,” the output of model#2 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, short-circuited faulty data of F7–F10 are utilized to train the SVM modle#3/PSVM model#3 to categorize “F7–F9” faults from “F10” fault. For demonstrating the triple line short-circuited fault conditions (F10), the output of SVM model#3/PSVM model#3 is represented by -1; otherwise 1 for F7–F9 condition. Thereafter, double line short-circuited faulty dataset of F7–F9 faults are utilized to train the SVM model#4/PSVM model#4 to categorize the “F7 and F8” faults from “F9” fault. For demonstrating “F9” fault condition, the output of SVM model#4/PSVM model#4 is represented by -1; otherwise 1. Now, “F7 and F8” faults dataset are utilized to train the SVM model#5/PSVM model#5 to categorize “F7” fault condition from “F8” fault. For demonstrating “F8” fault condition, the output of SVM model#5/PSVM model#5 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. In this way, FDD of short-circuited line condition has been completed. ¶ Now further classification of ground fault conditions, the ground fault dataset of (F1–F6) faults is utilized to train the SVM model#6/PSVM model#6 to categorize “F1–F3 (single line to ground)” faults from “F4–F6 (double line to ground)” faults. For demonstrating the “F4–F6” fault conditions, the output of SVM model#6/PSVM model#6 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Thereafter, double-line-to-ground fault dataset of F4-F6 faults are utilized to train the SVM model#7/PSVM model#7 to categorize “F6” fault condition from “(F4 and F5)” faults condition. For demonstrating “F6” fault condition, the output of SVM model#7/PSVM model#7 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Now, the faulty dataset of F4 and F5 are utilized to train the SVM model#8/PSVM model#8 to categorize “F5” fault from “F4” fault condition. For demonstrating “F5” fault condition, the output of SVM model#8/PSVM model#8 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Thereafter, single-line-to-ground fault dataset of F1-F3 are utilized to train the SVM model#9/PSVM model#9 to categorize “F1 & F2” fault form “F3” fault condition. For designating “F3” fault condition, the output of SVM model#9/PSVM model#9 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. Finally, F1 and F2 dataset are utilized to train the SVM model#10/PSVM model#10 to categorize “F1” fault from “F2” fault condition. For demonstrating the “F2” fault condition apart from “F1”, the output of SVM model#10/PSVM model#10 is represented by -1, otherwise 1. ¶ Hence, [Table 6.2](#B9780323855105000065.xhtml#t0015) represents the summary of output codification of all developed 10-SVM/PSVM models.
