# Chapter 10: Intelligent Data Analytics for Battery Health Forecasting Using Semi-Supervised and Unsupervised Extreme Learning Machines

## Abstract

Online health assessment of lithium-ion (Li-ion) battery (LIB) is required to predict the remaining useful life (RUL) of the battery-based system. Generally, internal resistance (R) and capacity (Ah) of the battery are utilized as a health identification feature (i.e., health indicator-HI). However, the measurement for both R and Ah are not an easy task, which leads to use of a large number of equipment and high analyzing cost. In this chapter, an alternative way to estimate the RUL during an online operating scenario has been formulated, which can extract the RUL from easy-recorded parameter of LIBs. To form a linear relationship between formulated HI and actual battery capacity, a power transformation method is applied and then Pearson and Spearman rank correlation methods are used to validate its performance. The supervised extreme learning machine (SELM), semi-supervised ELM (SSELM), and unsupervised ELM (USELM) are applied to evaluate the RUL by using extracted HI and transformed HI. This is the first attempt to implement the SSELM and USELM for RUL estimation of LIBs to make the system online in the real-time domain. The obtained results show that the new proposed approach is effective in evaluating the battery health condition without the need to measure the R and capacity.

## Keywords

health indicator remaining-useful-life (RUL) lithium-ion battery Box-Cox transformation artificial neural network online monitoring 

## 1. Introduction

Batteries are utilized for energy source and storage in several fields such as in electric vehicle (EV), communication, navigation, emergency back-up, aviation, and many more. According to the global patent report, the growth of research for the battery storage system can be analyzed based on the published number of patents by patent office globally during 2016–18 as shown in [Fig. 10.1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0010). [Fig. 10.1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0010) shows that the big player in the battery domains are from only three regions such as the United States patent and trademark office (US-PTO), European patent office (EPO), Chinese patent office (CPO), and rest of the world. The 9856 total number of patents have been granted by the patents offices in the world during 2016–18 for the R&D of battery energy storage, in which CPO contribute the highest number of patents around 43% followed by US-PTO (28% plus) and EPO (6.3% plus). Around 5368 patents have been filed within the Asian region, followed by North-America of around 2803 patents. According to the technology growth of battery storages, different types of inventions are going on day-by-day and a huge number of the invention are being filed to the patent offices throughout the globe. The seven different types of battery storage inventions are going on in the research domain as shown in [Fig. 10.2](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0015), such as DIB (dual-ion battery), Li-S (lithium-sulfur battery: LSB), Mg-Na (magnesium-based battery: MBB), Na-Ion (sodium-ion battery: SIB), K-Air (potassium-air battery: PAB), Ni-Zn (nickel-zinc battery: NZB), and Al-Air (aluminum-air battery (AAB). Therefore, the use of a particular type of battery storage depends on the different types of its properties (i.e., storage capacity, cost, performance, safety, charging/discharging, applicability, and level of technology, etc.) as shown in [Fig. 10.3](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0020). ![image](images/f10-01-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 10.1 Region-wise published patents in the area of battery storage globally \[ ![image](images/f10-02-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 10.2 File patents in the ongoing new research of the battery storage \[ ![image](images/f10-03-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 10.3 Different types of properties of the battery chemistries \[ According to [Fig. 10.3](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0020), among the different available technologies of battery, lithium-ion battery (LIB) is utilized widely in the world due to its superior properties, namely long life time, high energy density, light weight, low self-discharge rate, the clean environmental effect, and high output voltage \[ ![Figure 10.4 LIBs market demand globally \[ ¶ The repetitive charge/discharge condition deteriorates the battery performance gradually and it may lead to drastic failure. For example, NASA’s robotic spacecraft (Mars Global Surveyor) became a failure due to its battery failure in 2006 \[ ¶ In this regard, health assessment and RUL estimation can be performed easily by using capacity or internal resistance as an indicator. However, the online measurement of these two variables is very difficult. This is because the battery’s internal state variables are inaccessible to sensors under operational conditions and direct and accurate measurement of internal parameters is very costly, which makes the online condition monitoring utilizing these parameters ineffective. Therefore, non-intrusive extraction of health indicators from the commonly measured variables, which may reflect the health condition of the battery in online mode has become significant. ¶ In this chapter, a health indicator (HI) is extracted by using charge and discharge condition measurements. After extraction, optimization is performed to enhance the linear correlation between extracted HI and actual health condition of the battery. Now, transformed HI is used to find out the health condition instead of the actual condition, which allows assessing the online health estimation of the battery non-intrusively. ¶ The organization of this chapter is comprised of four sections. Section 1 represents the introduction and motivation part of the study. Section 2 explains the methodology part, which includes the experimental dataset collection, proposed approach framework, HI extraction, and its performance evaluation methods, RUL prediction techniques (SELM, SSELM, and USELM) and involved steps in the proposed approach. Section 3 presents the results and its discussion and experimental validation of all steps as mentioned in the proposed approach. Finally, Section 4 presents the conclusion of the study. ¶  ¶  ¶ 2. Methodology ¶ 2.1. Data collection for study ¶  ¶ For the experimentation validation of the proposed approach, the LIBs datasets collected from NASA \[ ¶ 1. Charging process: Charging experiment was performed in a constant current (CC) mode at 1.5 A until the battery voltage reached 4.2 V and then continued in a constant voltage (CV) mode until the charge current dropped to 20 mA.2. Discharging process: Discharge experiment was performed at a CC level of 2 A until the battery voltage fell to 2.7, 2.5, 2.2, and 2.5 V for batteries #5–#7, and #18, respectively.3. Impedance measurement: Impedance experiment was performed through an EIS frequency sweep from 0.1 Hz to 5 kHz. Repeated charge/discharge experiment result in accelerated aging of the batteries while impedance experiment provide insight into the internal battery parameters that change as aging progresses. ¶ Repetitive charging/discharging operation was stopped when the batteries reached end-of-life (EOL) criteria, which was 30% fade in rated capacity (from 2 to 1.4 Ah). The recorded dataset is utilized for HI extraction and multi-ELM based RUL estimation. The recorded qualities during both charging and discharging conditions have been represented in Figs. 10.5–10.9 and Figs. 10.10–10.14, respectively. ¶ Figure 10.5 Terminal voltage versus time representation during charging condition. ¶ Figure 10.6 Current measured versus time representation during charging condition. ¶ Figure 10.7 Battery temperature and time relationship. ¶ Figure 10.8 Output current versus time representation during charging condition. ¶ Figure 10.9 Voltage measured versus time representation during charging condition. ¶ Figure 10.10 Voltage measured versus time representation during discharging condition. ¶ Figure 10.11 Voltage versus time representation during discharging condition. ¶ Figure 10.12 Output current versus time representation during discharging condition. ¶ Figure 10.13 Current measured at load versus time representation during discharging condition. ¶ Figure 10.14 Temperature versus time representation during discharging condition. ¶  ¶ 2.2. Proposed approach framework ¶ Fig. 10.15 represents the proposed approach and methodology, which includes a complete procedure for HI extraction, HI optimization, HI validation, and finally RUL evaluation for LIBs. This scheme utilized the raw experimental datasets for the extraction and optimization of HI. Initially, trends of each recorded parameters have been analyzed to extract the HI then a correlation is performed to map the extracted HI with battery capacity. The presented scheme is applicable for online non-intrusive health assessment of LIBs, which is not possible by measurement of R and capacity of battery directly. In sub-section#1 (HI extraction), real-time dataset collection, and HI extraction are presented. For HI extraction, indirect HIs are extracted for the estimation of RUL for LIBs. After this, HI optimization is performed to enhance the correlation between extracted HI (eHI) and the actual capacity of the battery. Box-Cox transformation method (BCTM) used to enhance the correlation and its performance is validated by using Pearson & Sparceman correlation method. After getting optimized HI, the dataset is divided into two part for training and testing purposes. In Section 3, multiple ELMs are developed, trained, and tested one by one. The design and development for all ELMs approaches are based on Refs. \[ ¶ Figure 10.15 Approach for HI and RUL estimation. ¶  ¶ 2.2.1. Formulation of HI extraction and optimization ¶ 2.2.1.1. HI extraction ¶  ¶ For online non-intrusive health assessment of LIB, HI attribute should be easy to obtain during the operating scenarios. For LIB, the operating time, temperature, voltage, and current are easy to measure under charging and discharging conditions during live operation of the battery. So, HI can be extracted from the charging as well as discharging dataset of the battery. After analyzing Fig. 10.10, it is concluded that this curve is very similar to the deterioration curve. Discharging condition voltage has also physical significance with the degradation of battery capacity \[ ¶ Figure 10.16 Degradation based RUL estimation. ¶ ¶ \\left. {TI\_{(i)}} \\right|\_{DVI} = \\left| {T\_{(i)DV\\max} - T\_{(i)DV\\min}} \\right| ¶ image(10.1) ¶ where, T\_{(i)DV\\max}image= time at high voltage magnitude, T\_{(i)DV\\min}image= time at low voltage magnitude, and i = cycle number. ¶ Now, a vector of these calculated TI for 168 cycle of discharging condition has been formulated as: ¶ TI\_{Raw} = {\\lbrack TI\_{(1)},TI\_{(2)},TI\_{(3)},.......,TI\_{(i)}\\rbrack}^{T} ¶ image(10.2) ¶ This vector \[Eq. (10.2)\] is used as a HI for RUL assessment of the battery. ¶  ¶ 2.2.1.2. HI optimization using Box-Cox transformation and its parameter identification ¶ The curve obtained from vector of Eq. (10.2) has similar geometric characteristics of battery capacity, but it needs to be enhanced for a better correlation between them. For this, HI optimization is performed by Box-Cox (BC) transformation \[ ¶ Generally, BC transformation is a family of power transformation. It modifies normally distributed dataset to a set of data that has approximately normal distribution. The BC is represented mathematically as: ¶ TI\_{Raw}(\\lambda) = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix}
{\\frac{T{I\_{Raw}}^{\\lambda} - 1}{\\lambda}\\, if\\lambda \\neq 0} \\\\
{\\log(TI\_{Raw})\\, if\\lambda = 0\\,} \\\\
\\end{matrix} \\right. ¶ image(10.3) ¶ where, logarithm is the natural logarithm (log base e). The algorithm calls for finding the λ value that maximizes the log-likelihood function (LLF). Here, BC has been implemented by using R software then improvement of correlation between HI and battery capacity has been evaluated by PCA and SCA methods. ¶ The PCA evaluates the linear dependence (r) (linear relationship) between TI and capacity which varies from +1 to −1 and represents the significant of correlation as r = 1, r = 0, and r = −1 for positive linear, no linear, and negative linear correlation, respectively. Pearson correlation coefficient (r): ¶ r = \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(TI\_{i} - \\overline{TI})\*(Ah\_{i} - \\overline{Ah})}}{\\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(TI\_{i} - \\overline{TI})}^{2}}\\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah\_{i} - \\overline{Ah})}^{2}}} ¶ image(10.4) ¶ where, Ahimage = actual capacity, \\overline{Ah}image = mean value of Ahimage. ¶ SCA is a nonparametric measure of rank correlation using monotonic function (whether linear or not). A perfect SCA is represented in term of +1 or −1, if one variable is perfectly monotonic function to the other variable. ¶  ¶ 2.2.1.3. HI performance evaluation ¶ Here, HI performance evaluation is performed by using root mean squared (0 \\leq \\% RMSE \\leq 0.5)image and degree of fitness (0 < R^{2} \\leq 1)image, which are evaluated as Eq. (10.5) and Eq. (10.6), respectively. ¶ ¶ RMSE = \\sqrt{\\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\hat{Ah})}^{2}}{n}} ¶ image(10.5) ¶ ¶ R^{2} = 1 - \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\hat{Ah})}^{2}}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\overline{Ah})}^{2}} ¶ image(10.6) ¶ Where, \\hat{Ah} = \\text{estimated\\ capacity\\ after\\ transformation}image ¶  ¶  ¶ 2.2.2. RUL evaluation using multi-ELM ¶ 2.2.2.1. RUL prediction ¶  ¶ The main component of a predictive maintenance strategy is to find out the RUL of equipment. RUL is the time length of healthy operation without/before repair/replacement of the equipment. So, equipment maintenance can be scheduled properly by estimation of RUL. Generally, RUL of LIB can be predicted by a model-driven method and data-driven method. The common way/models based on data-driven methods to estimate the RUL are: (1) similarity model, (2) survival model, and (3) degradation model, which depends on a different kind of dataset such as: (1) lifetime data, (2) run-to-failure histories dataset, and (3) known threshold value of specific condition data, respectively \[ ¶ 2.2.2.2. Mathematical modeling of ELMs for RUL evaluation ¶ The ELM approach can be implemented for the both type of problems supervised (labelled dataset) and unsupervised (unlabelled dataset). For the labelled dataset, standard ELM (SELM) approach is implemented whereas for the unlabelled dataset, unsupervised ELM (USELM) approach is implemented in a simple way which is similar to spectral clustering (SC) and Laplacian eigenmaps (LE). However, USELM can be implemented straightforward to handle any data without evaluating eigenvectors. Moreover, if data set is the mixture of labelled and unlabelled dataset then semi-supervised ELM (SSELM) is implemented. The SSELM is based on SS learning algorithms (i.e., Laplacian regularized least squares-LapRLS, Laplacian SVMs-LapSVMs, SS deep embedding-SSDE, SS neural networks-SSNNs). The design and implementation of SELM, SSELM, and USEM are based on Ref. \[ ¶ 2.2.2.2.1. Mathematical modeling of SELM ¶ Let’s assume training labelled data: ¶ \\lbrack S,T\\rbrack = {\\lbrack s\_{i},t\_{i}\\rbrack}\_{i = 1}^{N} ¶ image(10.7) ¶ Where s\_{i} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{i}}image, t\_{i} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{1}}image for classification and t\_{i} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{0}}image for regression problem, t\_{i}image = binary dimension vector. ¶ The training procedure for SELM includes two general steps as follows: ¶ Step#1) To construct the hidden layer using randomly generated neurons using: ¶ Sigmoid function, ¶ z(s,\\lambda) = \\frac{1}{1 - \\exp( - (a^{T}s + b))} ¶ image(10.8) ¶ Gaussian function, ¶ z(s,\\lambda) = \\exp( - b{\\parallel {s - a} \\parallel}). ¶ image(10.9) ¶ Where, \\lambda = \\lbrack a,b\\rbrackimage = parameters and \\parallel \\text{·} \\parallelimage = Euclidean norm. ¶ In SELM, as per continuous probability distribution, parameters of hidden mapping function are generated randomly. In this step, hidden neurons map the training data into n\_{h}image feature space and represented as: ¶ Output of hidden layer with respect to s\_{i}image, ¶ h(s\_{i}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{1 \\times n\_{h}} ¶ image(10.10) ¶ Output weight (used to connect hidden layer & output layer), ¶ \\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}} ¶ image(10.11) ¶ Then output of the network, ¶ O(s\_{i}) = h(s\_{i})\\omega ¶ image(10.12) ¶ ¶ Step#2) Optimize the output weights by minimizing the error ¶ ¶ \\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\underset{- - - PART - 1 - - -}{\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2}} + \\underset{- - - - - PART - 2 - - - - -}{\\frac{C}{2}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}{\\parallel e\_{i} \\parallel}^{2}} ¶ image(10.13) ¶ ¶ s.t.\\, h(s\_{i})\\omega = t\_{i}^{T} - e\_{i}^{T} ¶ image(10.14) ¶ Where, PART-1 = regularization term against over-fitting, e\_{i} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{O}}image= error of ith training sample, C = penalty factor for e. ¶ Subtract the constraints form objective function, Eq. (10.13): ¶ \\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}J\_{ELM} = \\underset{- - - PART - 1 - - -}{\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2}} + \\underset{- - - - - - - PART - 2 - - - - - - -}{\\frac{C}{2}{\\parallel {T - P\\omega} \\parallel}^{2}} ¶ image(10.15) ¶ Where, ¶ P = {\\lbrack h{(s\_{i})}^{T},....,h{(s\_{N})}^{T}\\rbrack}^{T} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{N \\times n\_{h}} ¶ image(10.16) ¶ Now equating gradient of J\_{ELM}image with respect to \\omegaimage to zero as: ¶ \\nabla J\_{ELM} = \\omega + CP^{T}(T - P\\omega) = 0 ¶ image(10.17) ¶ If s > h\_{n}image (i.e., P has more row than column), the Eq. (10.15) will be: ¶ \\omega^{\*} = \\left( {P^{T}P + \\frac{L\_{n\_{h}}}{C}} \\right)^{- 1}P^{T}T ¶ image(10.18) ¶ where L\_{n\_{h}}image = identity matrix. ¶ if s < h\_{n}image (i.e., P has more column than row), the Eq. (10.15) will be: ¶ \\omega^{\*} = P^{T}\\beta^{\*} = P^{T}\\left( {PP^{T} + \\frac{L\_{N}}{C}} \\right)^{- 1}T ¶ image(10.19) ¶ Where, ¶ \\beta + C\\left( {T - PP^{T}\\beta} \\right) = 0 ¶ image(10.20) ¶ Therefore, the optimized output weight (\\omegaimage) will be evaluated as per the number of training data samples \\left( s\_{i} \\right)image and hidden layer neurons \\left( h\_{n} \\right)image by using Eqs. (10.18) and (10.19). ¶ 2.2.2.2.2. Mathematical modeling of SSELM ¶ The SSELM is helpful to analyze the mix dataset of both labelled and unlabelled dataset. Let’s assume: ¶ \\text{Labelled\\ dataset:}\\,\\lbrack S\_{l},T\_{l}\\rbrack = {\\lbrack x\_{i},t\_{i}\\rbrack}\_{i = 1}^{l} ¶ image(10.21) ¶ ¶ \\text{Unlabelled\\ dataset:}\\, S\_{u} = {\\lbrack x\_{i}\\rbrack}\_{i = 1}^{l} ¶ image(10.22) ¶ Where, l = labelled data samples and u = unlabelled data samples. ¶ After modification of SELM of Eqs. (10.19) and (10.20), SSELM can be represented as: ¶ \\begin{array}{l}
{\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{1}{2}C\_{i}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{l}{\\parallel e\_{i} \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{\\lambda}{2}Tr\\left( {H^{T}JH} \\right)} \\\\
{s.t.\\, h(s\_{i})\\omega = t\_{i}^{T} - e\_{i}^{T},\\, i = 1,..,l} \\\\
{\\, O\_{i} = h(s\_{i})\\omega,\\, i = 1,..,l + u} \\\\
\\end{array} ¶ image(10.23) ¶ Where, J \\in \\mathbb{R}^{(l + u) \\times (l + u)}image = graph Laplacian obtained from both type of datasets, H \\in \\mathbb{R}^{(l + u) \\times n\_{O}}image = output matrix for ith row which is o(s\_{i})image, and \\lambdaimage= tradeoff parameter (TP), C\_{i} = \\frac{C\_{o}}{N\_{t\_{i}}}image = penalty factor, C\_{o}image = parameter (user define), N\_{t\_{i}}image = training sample. ¶ After substituting the constraints, Eq. (10.23) is represented as: ¶ \\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel {C^{\\frac{1}{2}}\\left( {\\overset{\\sim}{T} - P\\omega} \\right)} \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{\\lambda}{2}Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right) ¶ image(10.24) ¶ Where, \\overset{\\sim}{T} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{(l + u) \\times n\_{O}}image= augmented training target for l rows ~ T\_{l}image (rest are zero). ¶ Now, value gradient of Eq. (10.24) with respect to \\omegaimage is ¶ \\nabla J\_{SSELM} = \\omega + P^{T}C\\left( {\\overset{\\sim}{T} - P\\omega} \\right) + \\lambda.P^{T}JP\\omega ¶ image(10.25) ¶ The output weight vector if s > h\_{n}image is ¶ \\omega\* = \\left( {L\_{n\_{h}} + P^{T}CP + \\lambda P^{T}JP} \\right)^{- 1}P^{T}C\\overset{\\sim}{T} ¶ image(10.26) ¶ The output weight vector if s < h\_{n}image is ¶ \\omega\* = P^{T}\\left( {L\_{l + u} + CPP^{T} + \\lambda JPP^{T}} \\right)^{- 1}C\\overset{\\sim}{T} ¶ image(10.27) ¶ 2.2.2.2.3. Mathematical modeling of USELM ¶ Assumed again unlabeled dataset similar to Eq. (10.22): S = {\\lbrack x\_{i}\\rbrack}\_{i = 1}^{N}image. ¶ Then formula for USELM is: ¶ \\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\lambda Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right) ¶ image(10.28) ¶ Eq. (10.22) can be represented as: ¶ \\begin{matrix}
{\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\lambda Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right)} \\\\
{\\, s.t.\\,{(P\\omega)}^{T}P\\omega = L\_{n\_{O}}} \\\\
\\end{matrix} ¶ image(10.29) ¶ The output weight vector if s > h\_{n}image is ¶ \\omega\* = \\lbrack{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{2},{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{3},..,{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{n\_{0} + 1}\\rbrack ¶ image(10.30) ¶ Where, normalized eigenvectors. ¶ ¶ {\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i} = \\left. {\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i}/{P{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i}} \\right.,\\,\\, i = 2,3,...,n\_{O} + 1 ¶ image(10.31) ¶ The output weight vector if s < h\_{n}image is ¶ \\omega\* = P^{T}\\lbrack{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{2},{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{3},..,{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{n\_{O} + 1}\\rbrack ¶ image(10.32) ¶ Where, normalized eigenvectors. ¶ ¶ {\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i} = \\left. {\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i}/{\\parallel {PP^{T}{{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i} \\parallel}}} \\right.,i = 2,3,...,n\_{O} + 1 ¶ image(10.33) ¶  ¶ 2.2.2.3. RUL performance analysis ¶ Here, multi-ELM-RUL model have been designed based on extracted HI from discharging dataset of battery number 5 and its performance is evaluated in term of absolute (AE), relative error (RE), mean absolute percent error (MAPE), and normalized RMSE as given: ¶ AE\_{RUL} = \\left| {A\_{RUL} - P\_{RUL}} \\right| ¶ image(10.34) ¶ ¶ RE\_{RUL} = \\left. \\left| {A\_{RUL} - P\_{RUL}} \\right|/A\_{RUL} \\right. ¶ image(10.35) ¶ ¶ MAPE\_{RUL} = \\left( {\\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\left| {A\_{RUL} - P\_{RUL}} \\right|}{\\left| A\_{RUL} \\right|}} \\right)\*100 ¶ image(10.36) ¶ ¶ NRMSE\_{RUL} = \\frac{RMSE\_{RUL}}{mean} ¶ image(10.37) ¶ Where, A\_{RUL}image = actual RUL, P\_{RUL}image = predicted RUL. ¶  ¶  ¶  ¶  ¶ 3. Results and discussion ¶ 3.1. HI extraction and optimization ¶ 3.1.1. HI extraction ¶  ¶ As mentioned in Section 2.2.1.1, HI should be easy in measurement and variable with respect to performance of battery. In this study, TI\_{Raw}image has been evaluated as per Eq. (10.1) and formulated in a series as per Eq. (10.2). The obtained series of TI\_{Raw}image is plotted for all four batteries (shown in Fig. 10.17) and compared with actual capacity of the battery (shown in Fig. 10.18). Here, TI\_{Raw}image is the time interval of discharging condition of the battery. ¶ Figure 10.17 Extracted HI degradation curve. ¶ Figure 10.18 Actual battery capacity curve. ¶ From Figs. 10.4 and 10.5, it can be concluded that both curve has similarity in pattern. For more validation point of view, a scatter plot has been simulated (shown in Fig. 10.19) between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery, which shows the close linear relationship. Though, there is a need for more improvement in linearity, which can be seen at top portion of the plotted curve. ¶ Figure 10.19 Scatter plot between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery. ¶ 3.1.2. HI optimization using Box-Cox transformation ¶ In order to improve linearity between extracted TI\_{Raw}image and actual capacity of battery (Ah), the BC transformation is applied by using R software to search the optimal value of \\lambdaimage. Here, lambda (\\lambdaimage) vary from range +5 to −5 with 1 stepping and a log-likelihood is plotted with 95% confidence interval as shown in Fig. 10.20. From the Fig. 10.20, it is concluded that \\lambdaimage = 1.1248 has highest performance. Now, a scatter plot is simulated (shown in Fig. 10.21) between optimized/transformed HI and actual capacity of battery, which shows the better linear relationship than Fig. 10.19. ¶ Figure 10.20 BC transformation for B#5. ¶ Figure 10.21 Correlation of TI and transformed capacity. ¶ 3.1.3. Transformed HI correlation analysis ¶ To analyze the significant improvement in the transformed HI through the BC transformation, Pearson and Spearman rank correlation is evaluated and obtained results are tabulated in Table 10.1. Furthermore, to represent the validity of the results shown in Table 10.1, the PCA coefficient is evaluated for all variation of \\lambdaimage from +5 to −5 (same value as used in BC transformation) and results are presented graphically in Fig. 10.22. From Fig. 10.22, it is concluded that PCA coefficient is higher at \\lambdaimage = 1.1248, which confirmed the reliability of likelihood method. The highest achieved PCA coefficient is 0.9999 (nearer to 1), which shows a very strong linear relationship between transformed HI and Ah. Moreover, SCA represents the relationship between transformed HI and Ah that HI is canonical enough to replace Ah for identifying the degradation of battery. For more verification, the correlation matrix between HI and Ah is presented in Figs. 10.23 and 10.24 for “before BC transformation” and “after BC transformation,” respectively. ¶ Table 10.1 ¶ TimePearson correlationSCA rankKendall rank correlation Before Box-Cox transformation0.999944890.999719070.994044 After Box-Cox transformation0.999985570.999820580.996738 ¶  ¶ Figure 10.22 PCA correlation coeffiecient with \\lambdaimage variation. ¶ Figure 10.23 Correlation matrix between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery #5. ¶ Figure 10.24 Correlation matrix between transformed HI and actual capacity of battery #5. ¶ 3.1.4. HI performance evaluation ¶ After all validation and evaluation as mentioned earlier, the actual capacity and estimated capacity results are presented in Fig. 10.25 for battery number 5–7 and 18. The HI performance evaluation is computed in term of RMSE, R2, MAPE, and NRMSE and tabulated in Table 10.2 for selected optimal value of lambda (\\lambdaimage) by BC transformation. From Table 10.2, it is concluded that R2 is very nearer to 1 for all battery cases, which means transformed capacity can be quantify the actual Ah value and hence, real-time degradation can be simulated by using extracted HI. ¶ Figure 10.25 Estimated capacity and actual capacity comparision representation for (A) Battery 5, (B) Battery 6, (C) Battery 7, and (D) Battery 18. ¶ Table 10.2 ¶ BatteryLambdaRMSER2MAPENRMSE B#51.12480.0019930.9999980.0986990.003502 B#61.06480.0043310.9999920.2192510.004913 B#71.23780.0094540.9999670.2489940.019272 B#181.07370.0223090.9997960.8104920.043407 ¶  ¶ 3.2. RUL estimation using ANN ¶ After analyzing the extracted and normalized HI, RUL evaluation is performed for battery dataset of NASA. The evaluation is carried out into three case studies as follow: ¶ 1. Case Study#1: RUL evaluation from raw HI as input and battery capacity as output to the model. This case study is carried out to validate the correlation of extracted HI with battery capacity.2. Case Study#2: RUL evaluation from raw HI as input and transformed HI as output to the model. This study is carried out to validate the consistency of the extracted HI framework.3. Case Study#3: RUL evaluation from transformed HI as input and battery capacity as output to the model. This study is carried out to evaluate the similarity of the extracted HI framework in evaluating the RUL. ¶ Now three multi-ELM-RUL models for three case studies have been formulated and tested after proper training & testing phase then RUL has been evaluated for different data samples starting from cycle number 20, 42, 122, 198, and 274 of battery 5. ¶ Before predicting the RUL, failure threshold of TI is calculated as it is 30% fade capacity rated from 2 to 1.4 Ah for battery number 5. Now BC transformed failure threshold capacity in term of time and cycle number are calculated, which are 2518 s and 124, respectively as shown in Figs. 10.26–10.29. ¶ Figure 10.26 Failure cycle estimation with EOL criterion. ¶ Figure 10.27 Threshold time and failure cycle estimation. ¶ Figure 10.28 TI failure threshold with respect to battery capacity. ¶ Figure 10.29 TI failure threshold with respect to BC transformed HI. ¶ After evaluating threshold value, ANN based RUL estimation is performed and model’s performance is evaluated at different dataset starting from cycle number 20, 42, 122, 198, and 274 of battery 5. The RUL forecasting accuracy is evaluated as per Eq. (10.34–10.37) and tabulated in Table 10.3. For better visualization, graphical representation has been shown in Fig. 10.30 for one case. ¶ Table 10.3 ¶  ¶  ¶ Case Study#2: SELM-SSELM-USELM-RUL model performance evaluation for B#5. ¶  ¶  ¶  ¶  ¶ Starting number](images/f10-04-9780323855105.jpg)

Figure 10.4 LIBs market demand globally \[ ¶ The repetitive charge/discharge condition deteriorates the battery performance gradually and it may lead to drastic failure. For example, NASA’s robotic spacecraft (Mars Global Surveyor) became a failure due to its battery failure in 2006 \[ ¶ In this regard, health assessment and RUL estimation can be performed easily by using capacity or internal resistance as an indicator. However, the online measurement of these two variables is very difficult. This is because the battery’s internal state variables are inaccessible to sensors under operational conditions and direct and accurate measurement of internal parameters is very costly, which makes the online condition monitoring utilizing these parameters ineffective. Therefore, non-intrusive extraction of health indicators from the commonly measured variables, which may reflect the health condition of the battery in online mode has become significant. ¶ In this chapter, a health indicator (HI) is extracted by using charge and discharge condition measurements. After extraction, optimization is performed to enhance the linear correlation between extracted HI and actual health condition of the battery. Now, transformed HI is used to find out the health condition instead of the actual condition, which allows assessing the online health estimation of the battery non-intrusively. ¶ The organization of this chapter is comprised of four sections. [Section 1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0010) represents the introduction and motivation part of the study. [Section 2](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0015) explains the methodology part, which includes the experimental dataset collection, proposed approach framework, HI extraction, and its performance evaluation methods, RUL prediction techniques (SELM, SSELM, and USELM) and involved steps in the proposed approach. [Section 3](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0085) presents the results and its discussion and experimental validation of all steps as mentioned in the proposed approach. Finally, [Section 4](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0120) presents the conclusion of the study. ¶ ¶ 

¶ 2. Methodology ¶ 2.1. Data collection for study ¶ 

¶ For the experimentation validation of the proposed approach, the LIBs datasets collected from NASA \[ ¶ 1. Charging process: Charging experiment was performed in a constant current (CC) mode at 1.5 A until the battery voltage reached 4.2 V and then continued in a constant voltage (CV) mode until the charge current dropped to 20 mA.2. Discharging process: Discharge experiment was performed at a CC level of 2 A until the battery voltage fell to 2.7, 2.5, 2.2, and 2.5 V for batteries #5–#7, and #18, respectively.3. Impedance measurement: Impedance experiment was performed through an EIS frequency sweep from 0.1 Hz to 5 kHz. Repeated charge/discharge experiment result in accelerated aging of the batteries while impedance experiment provide insight into the internal battery parameters that change as aging progresses. ¶ Repetitive charging/discharging operation was stopped when the batteries reached end-of-life (EOL) criteria, which was 30% fade in rated capacity (from 2 to 1.4 Ah). The recorded dataset is utilized for HI extraction and multi-ELM based RUL estimation. The recorded qualities during both charging and discharging conditions have been represented in [Figs. 10.5](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0030)–[10.9](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0050) and [Figs. 10.10](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0055)–[10.14](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0075), respectively. ¶ ![Figure 10.5 Terminal voltage versus time representation during charging condition.](images/f10-05-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.6 Current measured versus time representation during charging condition.](images/f10-06-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.7 Battery temperature and time relationship.](images/f10-07-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.8 Output current versus time representation during charging condition.](images/f10-08-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.9 Voltage measured versus time representation during charging condition.](images/f10-09-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.10 Voltage measured versus time representation during discharging condition.](images/f10-10-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.11 Voltage versus time representation during discharging condition.](images/f10-11-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.12 Output current versus time representation during discharging condition.](images/f10-12-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.13 Current measured at load versus time representation during discharging condition.](images/f10-13-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.14 Temperature versus time representation during discharging condition.](images/f10-14-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ¶ 2.2. Proposed approach framework ¶ [Fig. 10.15](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0080) represents the proposed approach and methodology, which includes a complete procedure for HI extraction, HI optimization, HI validation, and finally RUL evaluation for LIBs. This scheme utilized the raw experimental datasets for the extraction and optimization of HI. Initially, trends of each recorded parameters have been analyzed to extract the HI then a correlation is performed to map the extracted HI with battery capacity. The presented scheme is applicable for online non-intrusive health assessment of LIBs, which is not possible by measurement of R and capacity of battery directly. In sub-section#1 (HI extraction), real-time dataset collection, and HI extraction are presented. For HI extraction, indirect HIs are extracted for the estimation of RUL for LIBs. After this, HI optimization is performed to enhance the correlation between extracted HI (eHI) and the actual capacity of the battery. Box-Cox transformation method (BCTM) used to enhance the correlation and its performance is validated by using Pearson & Sparceman correlation method. After getting optimized HI, the dataset is divided into two part for training and testing purposes. In [Section 3](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0085), multiple ELMs are developed, trained, and tested one by one. The design and development for all ELMs approaches are based on Refs. \[ ¶ ![Figure 10.15 Approach for HI and RUL estimation.](images/f10-15-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ 

¶ 2.2.1. Formulation of HI extraction and optimization ¶ 2.2.1.1. HI extraction ¶ 

¶ For online non-intrusive health assessment of LIB, HI attribute should be easy to obtain during the operating scenarios. For LIB, the operating time, temperature, voltage, and current are easy to measure under charging and discharging conditions during live operation of the battery. So, HI can be extracted from the charging as well as discharging dataset of the battery. After analyzing [Fig. 10.10](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0055), it is concluded that this curve is very similar to the deterioration curve. Discharging condition voltage has also physical significance with the degradation of battery capacity \[ ¶ ![Figure 10.16 Degradation based RUL estimation.](images/f10-16-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ¶ *T**I*[(*i*)]|[*D**V**I*] = |*T*[(*i*)*D**V*max ]−*T*[(*i*)*D**V*min ]| ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si5.svg)(10.1) ¶ where, *T*[(*i*)*D**V*max ]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si6.svg)\= time at high voltage magnitude, *T*[(*i*)*D**V*min ]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si7.svg)\= time at low voltage magnitude, and i = cycle number. ¶ Now, a vector of these calculated TI for 168 cycle of discharging condition has been formulated as: ¶ *T**I*[*R**a**w*] = \[*T**I*[(1)], *T**I*[(2)], *T**I*[(3)], ......., *T**I*[(*i*)]\]^(*T*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si8.svg)(10.2) ¶ This vector \[Eq. [(10.2)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0015)\] is used as a HI for RUL assessment of the battery. ¶ ¶ 2.2.1.2. HI optimization using Box-Cox transformation and its parameter identification ¶ The curve obtained from vector of Eq. [(10.2)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0015) has similar geometric characteristics of battery capacity, but it needs to be enhanced for a better correlation between them. For this, HI optimization is performed by Box-Cox (BC) transformation \[ ¶ Generally, BC transformation is a family of power transformation. It modifies normally distributed dataset to a set of data that has approximately normal distribution. The BC is represented mathematically as: ¶ $TI\_{Raw}(\\lambda) = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\frac{T{I\_{Raw}}^{\\lambda} - 1}{\\lambda}\\, if\\lambda \\neq 0} \\\\ {\\log(TI\_{Raw})\\, if\\lambda = 0\\,} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si9.svg)(10.3) ¶ where, logarithm is the natural logarithm (log base e). The algorithm calls for finding the λ value that maximizes the log-likelihood function (LLF). Here, BC has been implemented by using R software then improvement of correlation between HI and battery capacity has been evaluated by PCA and SCA methods. ¶ The PCA evaluates the linear dependence (r) (linear relationship) between TI and capacity which varies from +1 to −1 and represents the significant of correlation as r = 1, r = 0, and r = −1 for positive linear, no linear, and negative linear correlation, respectively. Pearson correlation coefficient (r): ¶ $r = \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(TI\_{i} - \\overline{TI})\*(Ah\_{i} - \\overline{Ah})}}{\\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(TI\_{i} - \\overline{TI})}^{2}}\\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah\_{i} - \\overline{Ah})}^{2}}}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si10.svg)(10.4) ¶ where, *A**h*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si11.svg) = actual capacity, $\\overline{Ah}$![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si12.svg) = mean value of *A**h*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si13.svg). ¶ SCA is a nonparametric measure of rank correlation using monotonic function (whether linear or not). A perfect SCA is represented in term of +1 or −1, if one variable is perfectly monotonic function to the other variable. ¶ ¶ 2.2.1.3. HI performance evaluation ¶ Here, HI performance evaluation is performed by using root mean squared (0 ≤ %*R**M**S**E* ≤ 0.5)![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si14.svg) and degree of fitness (0 < *R*² ≤ 1)![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si15.svg), which are evaluated as Eq. [(10.5)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0030) and Eq. [(10.6)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0035), respectively. ¶ ¶ $RMSE = \\sqrt{\\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\hat{Ah})}^{2}}{n}}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si16.svg)(10.5) ¶ ¶ $R^{2} = 1 - \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\hat{Ah})}^{2}}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{(Ah - \\overline{Ah})}^{2}}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si17.svg)(10.6) ¶ Where, $\\hat{Ah} = \\text{estimated\\ capacity\\ after\\ transformation}$![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si18.svg) ¶ ¶ 

¶ 2.2.2. RUL evaluation using multi-ELM ¶ 2.2.2.1. RUL prediction ¶ 

¶ The main component of a predictive maintenance strategy is to find out the RUL of equipment. RUL is the time length of healthy operation without/before repair/replacement of the equipment. So, equipment maintenance can be scheduled properly by estimation of RUL. Generally, RUL of LIB can be predicted by a model-driven method and data-driven method. The common way/models based on data-driven methods to estimate the RUL are: (1) similarity model, (2) survival model, and (3) degradation model, which depends on a different kind of dataset such as: (1) lifetime data, (2) run-to-failure histories dataset, and (3) known threshold value of specific condition data, respectively \[ ¶ 2.2.2.2. Mathematical modeling of ELMs for RUL evaluation ¶ The ELM approach can be implemented for the both type of problems supervised (labelled dataset) and unsupervised (unlabelled dataset). For the labelled dataset, standard ELM (SELM) approach is implemented whereas for the unlabelled dataset, unsupervised ELM (USELM) approach is implemented in a simple way which is similar to spectral clustering (SC) and Laplacian eigenmaps (LE). However, USELM can be implemented straightforward to handle any data without evaluating eigenvectors. Moreover, if data set is the mixture of labelled and unlabelled dataset then semi-supervised ELM (SSELM) is implemented. The SSELM is based on SS learning algorithms (i.e., Laplacian regularized least squares-LapRLS, Laplacian SVMs-LapSVMs, SS deep embedding-SSDE, SS neural networks-SSNNs). The design and implementation of SELM, SSELM, and USEM are based on Ref. \[ ¶ 2.2.2.2.1. Mathematical modeling of SELM ¶ Let’s assume training labelled data: ¶ \[*S*, *T*\] = \[*s*[*i*], *t*[*i*]\][*i* = 1]^(*N*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si19.svg)(10.7) ¶ Where *s*[*i*] ∈ ℝ^(*n*[*i*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si20.svg), *t*[*i*] ∈ ℝ^(*n*[1])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si21.svg) for classification and *t*[*i*] ∈ ℝ^(*n*[0])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si22.svg) for regression problem, *t*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si23.svg) = binary dimension vector. ¶ The training procedure for SELM includes two general steps as follows: ¶ Step#1) To construct the hidden layer using randomly generated neurons using: ¶ Sigmoid function, ¶ $z(s,\\lambda) = \\frac{1}{1 - \\exp( - (a^{T}s + b))}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si24.svg)(10.8) ¶ Gaussian function, ¶ *z*(*s*, *λ*) = exp ( − *b* ∥ *s* − *a*∥). ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si25.svg)(10.9) ¶ Where, *λ* = \[*a*, *b*\]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si26.svg) = parameters and  ∥ ·∥![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si27.svg) = Euclidean norm. ¶ In SELM, as per continuous probability distribution, parameters of hidden mapping function are generated randomly. In this step, hidden neurons map the training data into *n*[*h*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si28.svg) feature space and represented as: ¶ Output of hidden layer with respect to *s*[*i*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si29.svg), ¶ *h*(*s*[*i*]) ∈ ℝ^(1 × *n*[*h*]) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si30.svg)(10.10) ¶ Output weight (used to connect hidden layer & output layer), ¶ *ω* ∈ ℝ^(*n*[*h*] × *n*[*O*]) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si31.svg)(10.11) ¶ Then output of the network, ¶ *O*(*s*[*i*]) = *h*(*s*[*i*])*ω* ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si32.svg)(10.12) ¶ ¶ Step#2) Optimize the output weights by minimizing the error ¶ ¶ $\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\underset{- - - PART - 1 - - -}{\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2}} + \\underset{- - - - - PART - 2 - - - - -}{\\frac{C}{2}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}{\\parallel e\_{i} \\parallel}^{2}}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si33.svg)(10.13) ¶ ¶ *s*.*t*. *h*(*s*[*i*])*ω* = *t*[*i*]^(*T*) − *e*[*i*]^(*T*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si34.svg)(10.14) ¶ Where, PART-1 = regularization term against over-fitting, *e*[*i*] ∈ ℝ^(*n*[*O*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si35.svg)\= error of i^(th) training sample, C = penalty factor for e. ¶ Subtract the constraints form objective function, Eq. [(10.13)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0070): ¶ $\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}J\_{ELM} = \\underset{- - - PART - 1 - - -}{\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2}} + \\underset{- - - - - - - PART - 2 - - - - - - -}{\\frac{C}{2}{\\parallel {T - P\\omega} \\parallel}^{2}}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si36.svg)(10.15) ¶ Where, ¶ *P* = \[*h*(*s*[*i*])^(*T*), ...., *h*(*s*[*N*])^(*T*)\]^(*T*) ∈ ℝ^(*N* × *n*[*h*]) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si37.svg)(10.16) ¶ Now equating gradient of *J*[*E**L**M*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si38.svg) with respect to *ω*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si39.svg) to zero as: ¶ ∇*J*[*E**L**M*] = *ω* + *C**P*^(*T*)(*T* − *P**ω*) = 0 ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si40.svg)(10.17) ¶ If *s* > *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si41.svg) (i.e., P has more row than column), the Eq. [(10.15)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0080) will be: ¶ $\\omega^{\*} = \\left( {P^{T}P + \\frac{L\_{n\_{h}}}{C}} \\right)^{- 1}P^{T}T$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si43.svg)(10.18) ¶ where *L*[*n*[*h*]]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si42.svg) = identity matrix. ¶ if *s* < *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si44.svg) (i.e., P has more column than row), the Eq. [(10.15)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0080) will be: ¶ $\\omega^{\*} = P^{T}\\beta^{\*} = P^{T}\\left( {PP^{T} + \\frac{L\_{N}}{C}} \\right)^{- 1}T$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si45.svg)(10.19) ¶ Where, ¶ *β* + *C*(*T*−*P**P*^(*T*)*β*) = 0 ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si46.svg)(10.20) ¶ Therefore, the optimized output weight (*ω*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si47.svg)) will be evaluated as per the number of training data samples (*s*[*i*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si48.svg) and hidden layer neurons (*h*[*n*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si49.svg) by using Eqs. [(10.18)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0095) and [(10.19)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0100). ¶ 2.2.2.2.2. Mathematical modeling of SSELM ¶ The SSELM is helpful to analyze the mix dataset of both labelled and unlabelled dataset. Let’s assume: ¶ Labelled dataset: \[*S*[*l*], *T*[*l*]\] = \[*x*[*i*], *t*[*i*]\][*i* = 1]^(*l*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si50.svg)(10.21) ¶ ¶ Unlabelled dataset: *S*[*u*] = \[*x*[*i*]\][*i* = 1]^(*l*) ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si51.svg)(10.22) ¶ Where, l = labelled data samples and u = unlabelled data samples. ¶ After modification of SELM of Eqs. [(10.19)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0100) and [(10.20)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0105), SSELM can be represented as: ¶ $\\begin{array}{l} {\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{1}{2}C\_{i}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{l}{\\parallel e\_{i} \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{\\lambda}{2}Tr\\left( {H^{T}JH} \\right)} \\\\ {s.t.\\, h(s\_{i})\\omega = t\_{i}^{T} - e\_{i}^{T},\\, i = 1,..,l} \\\\ {\\, O\_{i} = h(s\_{i})\\omega,\\, i = 1,..,l + u} \\\\ \\end{array}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si52.svg)(10.23) ¶ Where, *J* ∈ ℝ^((*l* + *u*) × (*l* + *u*))![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si53.svg) = graph Laplacian obtained from both type of datasets, *H* ∈ ℝ^((*l* + *u*) × *n*[*O*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si54.svg) = output matrix for i^(th) row which is *o*(*s*[*i*])![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si55.svg), and *λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si56.svg)\= tradeoff parameter (TP), $C\_{i} = \\frac{C\_{o}}{N\_{t\_{i}}}$![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si57.svg) = penalty factor, *C*[*o*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si58.svg) = parameter (user define), *N*[*t*[*i*]]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si59.svg) = training sample. ¶ After substituting the constraints, Eq. [(10.23)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0120) is represented as: ¶ $\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}\\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{1}{2}{\\parallel {C^{\\frac{1}{2}}\\left( {\\overset{\\sim}{T} - P\\omega} \\right)} \\parallel}^{2} + \\frac{\\lambda}{2}Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right)$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si60.svg)(10.24) ¶ Where, $\\overset{\\sim}{T} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{(l + u) \\times n\_{O}}$![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si61.svg)\= augmented training target for l rows ~ *T*[*l*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si62.svg) (rest are zero). ¶ Now, value gradient of Eq. [(10.24)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0125) with respect to *ω*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si63.svg) is ¶ $\\nabla J\_{SSELM} = \\omega + P^{T}C\\left( {\\overset{\\sim}{T} - P\\omega} \\right) + \\lambda.P^{T}JP\\omega$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si64.svg)(10.25) ¶ The output weight vector if *s* > *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si65.svg) is ¶ $\\omega\* = \\left( {L\_{n\_{h}} + P^{T}CP + \\lambda P^{T}JP} \\right)^{- 1}P^{T}C\\overset{\\sim}{T}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si66.svg)(10.26) ¶ The output weight vector if *s* < *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si67.svg) is ¶ $\\omega\* = P^{T}\\left( {L\_{l + u} + CPP^{T} + \\lambda JPP^{T}} \\right)^{- 1}C\\overset{\\sim}{T}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si68.svg)(10.27) ¶ 2.2.2.2.3. Mathematical modeling of USELM ¶ Assumed again unlabeled dataset similar to Eq. [(10.22)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0115): *S* = \[*x*[*i*]\][*i* = 1]^(*N*)![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si69.svg). ¶ Then formula for USELM is: ¶ $\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\lambda Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right)$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si70.svg)(10.28) ¶ Eq. [(10.22)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0115) can be represented as: ¶ $\\begin{matrix} {\\min\\limits\_{\\omega \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n\_{h} \\times n\_{O}}}{\\parallel \\omega \\parallel}^{2} + \\lambda Tr\\left( {\\omega^{T}P^{T}JP\\omega} \\right)} \\\\ {\\, s.t.\\,{(P\\omega)}^{T}P\\omega = L\_{n\_{O}}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si71.svg)(10.29) ¶ The output weight vector if *s* > *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si72.svg) is ¶ $\\omega\* = \\lbrack{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{2},{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{3},..,{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{n\_{0} + 1}\\rbrack$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si73.svg)(10.30) ¶ Where, normalized eigenvectors. ¶ ¶ ${\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i} = \\left. {\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i}/{P{\\overset{\\sim}{m}}\_{i}} \\right.,\\,\\, i = 2,3,...,n\_{O} + 1$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si74.svg)(10.31) ¶ The output weight vector if *s* < *h*[*n*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si75.svg) is ¶ $\\omega\* = P^{T}\\lbrack{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{2},{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{3},..,{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{n\_{O} + 1}\\rbrack$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si76.svg)(10.32) ¶ Where, normalized eigenvectors. ¶ ¶ ${\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i} = \\left. {\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i}/{\\parallel {PP^{T}{{\\overset{\\sim}{b}}\_{i} \\parallel}}} \\right.,i = 2,3,...,n\_{O} + 1$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si77.svg)(10.33) ¶ ¶ 2.2.2.3. RUL performance analysis ¶ Here, multi-ELM-RUL model have been designed based on extracted HI from discharging dataset of battery number 5 and its performance is evaluated in term of absolute (AE), relative error (RE), mean absolute percent error (MAPE), and normalized RMSE as given: ¶ *A**E*[*R**U**L*] = |*A*[*R**U**L*]−*P*[*R**U**L*]| ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si78.svg)(10.34) ¶ ¶ *R**E*[*R**U**L*] = |*A*[*R**U**L*]−*P*[*R**U**L*]|/*A*[*R**U**L*] ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si79.svg)(10.35) ¶ ¶ $MAPE\_{RUL} = \\left( {\\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\left| {A\_{RUL} - P\_{RUL}} \\right|}{\\left| A\_{RUL} \\right|}} \\right)\*100$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si80.svg)(10.36) ¶ ¶ $NRMSE\_{RUL} = \\frac{RMSE\_{RUL}}{mean}$ ¶ ![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si81.svg)(10.37) ¶ Where, *A*[*R**U**L*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si82.svg) = actual RUL, *P*[*R**U**L*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si83.svg) = predicted RUL. ¶ ¶ ¶ ¶ 

¶ 3. Results and discussion ¶ 3.1. HI extraction and optimization ¶ 3.1.1. HI extraction ¶ 

¶ As mentioned in [Section 2.2.1.1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#s0035), HI should be easy in measurement and variable with respect to performance of battery. In this study, *T**I*[*R**a**w*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si84.svg) has been evaluated as per Eq. [(10.1)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0010) and formulated in a series as per Eq. [(10.2)](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0015). The obtained series of *T**I*[*R**a**w*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si85.svg) is plotted for all four batteries (shown in [Fig. 10.17](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0090)) and compared with actual capacity of the battery (shown in [Fig. 10.18](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0095)). Here, *T**I*[*R**a**w*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si86.svg) is the time interval of discharging condition of the battery. ¶ ![Figure 10.17 Extracted HI degradation curve.](images/f10-17-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.18 Actual battery capacity curve.](images/f10-18-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ From [Figs. 10.4](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0025) and [10.5](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0030), it can be concluded that both curve has similarity in pattern. For more validation point of view, a scatter plot has been simulated (shown in [Fig. 10.19](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0100)) between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery, which shows the close linear relationship. Though, there is a need for more improvement in linearity, which can be seen at top portion of the plotted curve. ¶ ![Figure 10.19 Scatter plot between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery.](images/f10-19-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ 3.1.2. HI optimization using Box-Cox transformation ¶ In order to improve linearity between extracted *T**I*[*R**a**w*]![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si87.svg) and actual capacity of battery (Ah), the BC transformation is applied by using R software to search the optimal value of *λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si88.svg). Here, lambda (*λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si89.svg)) vary from range +5 to −5 with 1 stepping and a log-likelihood is plotted with 95% confidence interval as shown in [Fig. 10.20](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0105). From the [Fig. 10.20](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0105), it is concluded that *λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si90.svg) = 1.1248 has highest performance. Now, a scatter plot is simulated (shown in [Fig. 10.21](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0110)) between optimized/transformed HI and actual capacity of battery, which shows the better linear relationship than [Fig. 10.19](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0100). ¶ ![Figure 10.20 BC transformation for B#5.](images/f10-20-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.21 Correlation of TI and transformed capacity.](images/f10-21-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ 3.1.3. Transformed HI correlation analysis ¶ To analyze the significant improvement in the transformed HI through the BC transformation, Pearson and Spearman rank correlation is evaluated and obtained results are tabulated in [Table 10.1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#t0010). Furthermore, to represent the validity of the results shown in [Table 10.1](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#t0010), the PCA coefficient is evaluated for all variation of *λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si91.svg) from +5 to −5 (same value as used in BC transformation) and results are presented graphically in [Fig. 10.22](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0115). From [Fig. 10.22](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0115), it is concluded that PCA coefficient is higher at *λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si92.svg) = 1.1248, which confirmed the reliability of likelihood method. The highest achieved PCA coefficient is 0.9999 (nearer to 1), which shows a very strong linear relationship between transformed HI and Ah. Moreover, SCA represents the relationship between transformed HI and Ah that HI is canonical enough to replace Ah for identifying the degradation of battery. For more verification, the correlation matrix between HI and Ah is presented in [Figs. 10.23](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0120) and [10.24](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0125) for “before BC transformation” and “after BC transformation,” respectively. ¶ Table 10.1 ¶ TimePearson correlationSCA rankKendall rank correlation  
Before Box-Cox transformation0.999944890.999719070.994044  
After Box-Cox transformation0.999985570.999820580.996738 ¶ ![](images/B9780323855105000107-t0010.png)¶ ![Figure 10.22 PCA correlation coeffiecient with \\lambdaimage variation.](images/f10-22-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.23 Correlation matrix between extracted HI and actual capacity of battery #5.](images/f10-23-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.24 Correlation matrix between transformed HI and actual capacity of battery #5.](images/f10-24-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ 3.1.4. HI performance evaluation ¶ After all validation and evaluation as mentioned earlier, the actual capacity and estimated capacity results are presented in [Fig. 10.25](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0130) for battery number 5–7 and 18. The HI performance evaluation is computed in term of RMSE, R², MAPE, and NRMSE and tabulated in [Table 10.2](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#t0015) for selected optimal value of lambda (*λ*![image](images/B9780323855105000107-si93.svg)) by BC transformation. From [Table 10.2](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#t0015), it is concluded that R² is very nearer to 1 for all battery cases, which means transformed capacity can be quantify the actual Ah value and hence, real-time degradation can be simulated by using extracted HI. ¶ ![Figure 10.25 Estimated capacity and actual capacity comparision representation for (A) Battery 5, (B) Battery 6, (C) Battery 7, and (D) Battery 18.](images/f10-25-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ Table 10.2 ¶ BatteryLambdaRMSER2MAPENRMSE  
B#51.12480.0019930.9999980.0986990.003502  
B#61.06480.0043310.9999920.2192510.004913  
B#71.23780.0094540.9999670.2489940.019272  
B#181.07370.0223090.9997960.8104920.043407 ¶ ![](images/B9780323855105000107-t0015.png)¶ 3.2. RUL estimation using ANN ¶ After analyzing the extracted and normalized HI, RUL evaluation is performed for battery dataset of NASA. The evaluation is carried out into three case studies as follow: ¶ 1. Case Study#1: RUL evaluation from raw HI as input and battery capacity as output to the model. This case study is carried out to validate the correlation of extracted HI with battery capacity.2. Case Study#2: RUL evaluation from raw HI as input and transformed HI as output to the model. This study is carried out to validate the consistency of the extracted HI framework.3. Case Study#3: RUL evaluation from transformed HI as input and battery capacity as output to the model. This study is carried out to evaluate the similarity of the extracted HI framework in evaluating the RUL. ¶ Now three multi-ELM-RUL models for three case studies have been formulated and tested after proper training & testing phase then RUL has been evaluated for different data samples starting from cycle number 20, 42, 122, 198, and 274 of battery 5. ¶ Before predicting the RUL, failure threshold of TI is calculated as it is 30% fade capacity rated from 2 to 1.4 Ah for battery number 5. Now BC transformed failure threshold capacity in term of time and cycle number are calculated, which are 2518 s and 124, respectively as shown in [Figs. 10.26](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0135)–[10.29](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0150). ¶ ![Figure 10.26 Failure cycle estimation with EOL criterion.](images/f10-26-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.27 Threshold time and failure cycle estimation.](images/f10-27-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.28 TI failure threshold with respect to battery capacity.](images/f10-28-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ ![Figure 10.29 TI failure threshold with respect to BC transformed HI.](images/f10-29-9780323855105.jpg "fig:")¶ After evaluating threshold value, ANN based RUL estimation is performed and model’s performance is evaluated at different dataset starting from cycle number 20, 42, 122, 198, and 274 of battery 5. The RUL forecasting accuracy is evaluated as per Eq. ([10.34](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0175)–[10.37](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#e0190)) and tabulated in [Table 10.3](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#t0020). For better visualization, graphical representation has been shown in [Fig. 10.30](#B9780323855105000107.xhtml#f0155) for one case. ¶ Table 10.3 ¶ 

¶

## 4. Conclusion

In this chapter, a new HI formation and transformation approach for LIB health assessment is proposed. With the help of the power transformation method (named Box-Cox transformation), formulated HI is optimized to increase the linear relationship between extracted HI and the actual capacity of the battery. Thereafter, a linearized relationship is validated by two different approaches, which shows R² values near to one. Moreover, multi-ELM methods have been used to realize indirect RUL evaluation with raw extracted, optimized, and transformed HI. Obtained results in each case study satisfy the effectiveness of the proposed approach. In the future perspective, this approach can be enhanced with other HI variable. Moreover, the selection of the most relevant input variable for RUL assessment needs to be further analyzed.
